A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Climate rationale
Rwanda is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change and is increasingly experiencing the impacts of climate change. Current climate variability in Rwanda is already resulting in an increased incidence of droughts, floods, landslides, and other extreme weather events. These are causing damage to crops, livestock, roads and other infrastructure as well as cutting power cuts. The national determination contribution of Rwanda has set a target for the country to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by 30% below business-as-usual by 2030. Changes in temperature and precipitation and their distributions are the key drivers of climate and weather-related impacts on agriculture, water resources, health, infrastructure, and other sectors. The average annual mean temperature rise from 1971 to 2016 has between 1.4°C and 2.58°C in the south-west and eastern regions of Rwanda. In addition, there is a significant increase in rainfall intensity with an increase of up to 30% in some areas. This presents challenges for water management and storage to cope with the future water demand from Rwanda's growing urban population. The country has a total annual rainfall of about 165 cm per year and 29% of the population is still unable to access safe drinking water.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) in its 4th and 5th Assessment Reports identified climate change as one of the major drivers for urbanization. The Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources reported that that 62.0% of the urban population in Rwanda in 2016 was in informal settlements, while less than 20% of the urban population had access to basic services such as water supply and sanitation. It is clear that climate risks in cities cannot be addressed without the upgrade of informal settlements. In parallel, the upgrade of settlements will be incomplete without accounting for and incorporating the risks of climate change. Kigali, where official estimates

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

The project site is situated on Kinyinya Hill in Gasaabo district, 6.9 km to the northeast of the central business district of Kigali. The population of Gasaabo is ranked as one of the most vulnerable districts to landslides due to its high population density, the high concentration of informal settlements and the steep slopes on which it sits. The project site has a total area of 600ha, with approximately 7% of all the housing in Rwanda that is susceptible to landslides due to its high population density, the high concentration of informal settlements and the steep slopes on which it sits.

The project site resides within a larger 600ha area at Kinyinya Hill which has been designated by the GVB as a focal area for the development of communities employing urbanization models to provide affordable housing and infrastructure. The project site is located in the northern part of this area, where the GVB has established new standards that can be replicated elsewhere in Rwanda and beyond - setting a trajectory towards a Net Zero Emission Community (NZEC). This NZEC will be developed on the land between the existing informal settlement at Kinyinya Hill and the planned urbanization zone to the north. The project site is located within the western half of the 600ha area. This 600ha model area has been identified originally as the Green City Pilot and includes a mix of residential, commercial, institutional and public spaces that will provide housing for current and future cultural and environmental climate. Doing so engages with the challenges vulnerability to climate change and urbanization.

The upgrade of the informal settlement at Ngayinya has been identified as an informal settlement that is typical of its type in Kigali (formerly rural), then peri-urban and increasingly urban as the city of Kigali grows rapidly). Ngayinya, which is located within the planned urbanization zone to the north of the project site, is currently a high density settlement with approximately 70% of all the housing in Rwanda that is susceptible to landslides due to its high population density, the high concentration of informal settlements and the steep slopes on which it sits. The settlement is particularly vulnerable to present and future climate change threats as well as the other issues that affect a low income community such as poor sanitation, lack of access to clean water, inadequate waste management, etc.

The project site has been identified by the GVB as an area where the NZEC will be developed on the land between the existing informal settlement at Kinyinya Hill and the planned urbanization zone to the north. The project site is located within the western half of the 600ha area. This 600ha model area has been identified originally as the Green City Pilot and includes a mix of residential, commercial, institutional and public spaces that will provide housing for current and future cultural and environmental climate. Doing so engages with the challenges vulnerability to climate change and urbanization.

The upgrade of informal settlements within the frame of green city development is also pertinent to mitigation efforts. Under a business-as-usual projection, Rwanda's total emissions are forecast to more than double over the 2015-2030 period (from 48 million tonnes CO2e in 2015 to 97 million tonnes CO2e by 2030). This is due primarily to population growth and land use change. The forecast indicates the growing contribution from fossil fuels to national emissions, arising mainly from transport.

According to the nationally determined contribution (NDC) of Rwanda, a detailed assessment of identified GHG mitigation opportunities has been undertaken in order to identify those that are most cost-effective for implementation against the business as usual (BAU) emissions in the same year of 12.1 million tCO2e. The NDC presents an unconditional target of reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 30% below BAU levels and a conditional target of achieving mitigation level of 1.9 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) in that year. This is an unconditional target, based on the country's current capacity to implement mitigation measures and does not include any financial contribution: An additional reduction of 20 per cent relative to BAU in the year 2030, equivalent to an estimated emission reduction of 15 million tonnes CO2e, can be achieved through a combination of energy efficiency improvements and the provision of international support & funding.

Without action, increases in energy demand will continue to meet increasing energy demand dominates the mitigation potential. Significant emissions reduction potential exists across each of the main sub-sectors: Hydropower, covering large- and small-scale hydropower; solar water heating, which represents a significant opportunity for the deployment of solar energy for water heating, pumping for agricultural irrigation and off-grid electricity which together account for around a quarter of all mitigation.

The project supports activities under the conditional NDC target, Solar mini-grids, Offgrid and rooftop solar electrification, Solar water heater (SWH) programme.

Following the international sources, major sources for 2015 included CO2 emissions from fuel combustion for heating and cooking in buildings (LPG, kerosene), which accounted for 14% of the total, and CO2 emissions from liquid fuels used by transport.

Emissions from Nanyangwa Village are expected to follow similar trends under BAU scenario with increasing levels of energy consumption for cooking, lighting and operating electrical appliances is expected to increase. Emissions from transportation will also increase as more vehicles are acquired in the village. In the solid waste sector, the BAU scenarios includes increased emissions from solid waste disposal and wastewater.

There is a direct and well-documented link between gender equality, women's empowerment and climate change. Research shows that women are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts, particularly in developing countries where existing gender disparities become even more pronounced and harmful over time. Rapidly advancing climate change will exacerbate these inequalities and make it harder for women to adapt to the new realities of their communities hardest. Due to gender bias and inequalities related to education, economic empowerment, voice and access to resources, women are often less able to participate in decision-making processes that affect them.

A case of Rwanda. Among other disadvantaged groups who experience poverty, women are often the poorest of the poor. They have limited access to land, credit, education and training opportunities, and they earn lower wages than men. Women climate change as their incomes are often highly dependent on natural resources that are sensitive to climate shocks and changes in weather patterns. In addition, women tend to be more vulnerable to climate change because they have less economic independence, and are not involved in political or household decision-making processes to the same extent as men.

In order to ensure that climate change adaptation and mitigation interventions are effective and sustainable, the clear requirements of the GCF Gender Policy, it is critical that gender equality considerations and actions be integrated into all climate change adaptation and/or mitigation interventions.


## Proposed Interventions

The project at its core focuses on the sustainable upgrade of internal settlements by piloting a realistic climate mitigative and adaptive multi-sectoral program of actions that can then be used as a template for future upgrading at similar communities. The project will focus on the following key areas in order to ensure that the upgrading is sustainable, in order to facilitate this future upgrading expansion as well as to promote new sustainable greenfield housing developments in the future.

- Integrated investments in resilient infrastructure: household energy solutions and community facilities in an internal settlement on Kinyinya Hill - Nanyangwa Village, to serve as a model for a resilient, low emission upgrade.
The main components of this investment will be:
  * creating a green, permeable network of sewers, gutters and detention/filtration-percolation areas to increase coverage of stormwater management systems in the settlement and reduce flooding and soil erosion on old landfills;
  * establishing community composting neighbourhood collection points; recycling centers to improve solid waste management, reducing emissions, increasing the productivity of urban agriculture and reducing the blockage of drainage channels.
  * expanding the central water supply (including connections), rainwater harvesting point, use of household water filters and water conservation devices to reduce water consumption and increase water security;
  * creating green rights way networks to improve transport and mobility, safety and security, using green corridors and permeable pavements.
  * establishing toilets with biogas recovery and providing training on sanitation improvements to improve health and hygiene conditions in the settlement.
  * installing solar PV and solar water heaters (SWH) and promoting the use of clean cooking technology, energy efficient appliances and lighting systems for improved energy security and reduced emissions;
  * establishing a technical and vocational educational and training (TVET) centre; community focal points and market stalls to provide employment opportunities in the settlement.
- Provision of Technical Assistance (TA) to support the scale up and replication of resilient, low emission approaches through:
  * developing capacity building programs for building the capacity of construction sector workers and professionals to adopt best practices in green construction;
  * developing a model for sustainable housing construction that can be replicated in Rwanda
  * developing training events to enhance the institutional capacity of Gulf agencies to support inclusive, green development.
  * providing support relating to the planning and regulatory environment in order to develop an enabling environment that fosters increased investment in the green upgrade of internal settlements.

Climate impacts / Benefits

The result is a project with strong climate benefits, as well as social, health, economic, environmental co-benefits. The project will reduce CO2 emissions by 125,032 tCO2e over the lifetime of the investments (see Annex 2 for details), as well as adapting to present and future climate risks. This will contribute to the targets set out in the Paris Agreement which advances several of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including:

- **climate benefit specific in bold:**
    - SDG 1 no poverty
    - SDG 3 good health and well-being
    - SDG 5 gender equality
    - SDG 6 clean water and sanitation
    - SDG 7 affordable and clean energy
    - SDG 8 decent work and economic growth
    - SDG 9 industry, innovation and infrastructure
    - SDG 10 reduced inequalities
    - SDG 11 sustainable cities and communities
    - **SDG 12 responsible consumption and production**
    - SDG 13 climate action
    - SDG 17 partnerships for the goals

The project's expected results include:

- An upgraded urban settlement with public infrastructure that is low emission and resilient to flooding, landslides and other natural hazards; household energy use that is efficient, low emission and geared to low-income households to demonstrate innovation and best practice;
- Increased capacity of local authorities to adopt sustainable approaches and enhanced capacity across all stakeholder groups to support and participate in low emission and climate-resilient upgrade projects; and
- an enabling environment for the scaling up of similar urban settlement investments in urban settlements that will contribute to low-carbon pathways towards 2030 and beyond.

# B. Project/Programme Information

## B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Climate change problem

The project will address: 1) The high vulnerability and low resilience of informal urban settlements to climate change; and 2) the need for an alternative high emission development pathway that would ordinarily occur with the re-development of an informal urban settlement in Kigali.

#### 1.1 Climate vulnerability and low resilience

Climate change impacts in the Central African region (which includes Rwanda) are significant, as the region's climate is already changing. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) reports that the central African region has experienced an increase of mean annual temperature by 0.75°C-1.2°C since 1960.

##### Current climate variability in Rwanda

Current climate variability in Rwanda is already resulting in an increased incidence of drought, flooding and extreme weather events such as heatwaves, heavy rainfall and storms. These are expected to increase with the impacts of climate change. The national meteorological service reports that the country has experienced a decrease in precipitation since 1960.

##### Projected changes

The IPCC projects that mean annual temperatures in Central Africa are projected to be an additional 2°C by 2050, and up to 4°C by 2100. Under a 1.5°C warming scenario, the region is expected to experience an increase of mean annual temperature by 3.7°C-6.8°C.

##### Projected changes in precipitation

The IPCC further reports that under temperature scenarios of 1.5°C - 2°C and above, there will be a 24% temperature increase scenario. The IPCC further reports that under temperature scenarios of 1.5°C – 2°C and above, there will be an increase in mean annual precipitation by up to 30%. This is projected to increase the likelihood of widespread flood occurrences before, during and after heavy rainfall.

##### Projected changes in extreme weather events

The IPCC further reports that under temperature scenarios of 1.5°C – 2°C and above, there will be an increase in mean annual precipitation by up to 30%. This is projected to increase the likelihood of widespread flood occurrences before, during and after heavy rainfall.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

### Rwanda’s high level of urbanization increases its overall vulnerability to climate change. Urban households are vulnerable to climate change due to Rwanda's flat topography and the high number of people living in informal settlements.

Low infiltration rates for storm water create high runoff levels leading to erosion and downstream flooding, which is exacerbated by the lack of proper drainage systems and poor storm water considerations in 2016[1]. Moreover, low recharge rates for groundwater tables reduce the availability of groundwater resources. The country has experienced a significant increase in extreme weather events over recent years.

Many informal settlements are concentrated on sites most at risk from flooding and landslides with poor quality housing and infrastructure less able to withstand extreme weather events. Informal settlements tend to be located on steep slopes, which increases their vulnerability to landslides during heavy rainfall. These areas also have a higher population density than urban areas, increasing the number of people exposed to hazards that contribute to higher indoor temperatures. Impacts from rising maximum temperatures, more hot days and heat waves are expected to increase in Rwanda over the coming decades.

Rising minimum temperatures with fewer cold days is also extending the range and activity of some disease vectors including mosquitoes which can transmit malaria and dengue fever[2]. The country has experienced a 0.5°C warming since 1950[3]. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) in its 4th and 5th assessments have explicitly identified Rwanda as one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change.

### Residents in informal settlements experience greater water constraints during prolonged dry spells. Periods of water stress arise due to a low capture and storage capacity and a high precipitation run-off rate[4]. Water demand is increasing with urbanization, population growth and economic development. Most households are connected to mains water supply (only 30% of households in Kigali have piped water into the home), industries develop their own water systems and agriculture relies on rainfed irrigation.

The high concentration of informal settlements in vulnerable areas increases the risk of landslides, which create heat stress and new challenges for water management and storage to cope with the future water demand from Rwanda’s growing population. The country has a low per capita access to safe drinking water (70% of water per capita per year) and 25% of the population are still unable to access safe drinking water.

There are an estimated 29,215 houses across the country that are vulnerable to landslides. The exposure of informal settlements is high due to their location on steep slopes and poor drainage systems as well as the concentration of informal settlements and use of low-quality construction materials. At three districts in the City of Kigali, the population density exceeds 20,000 people per km².

The population of Gasabo is ranked as one of the most vulnerable districts to landslides in Rwanda (second only to Nyamagabe). The district has a high population density and over 75% of its land area contains 7% or all the housing in Rwanda that is susceptible to landslides due to its high population density, the high concentration of informal settlements and use of low-quality construction materials.

The Kinyinya Hill site extends over approximately 600 ha with a population of 168,952 and is a typical hillside to urban development. The area has experienced rapid urbanization in recent years due to its proximity to the city centre and more under development or consideration. There are a number of informal settlements on Kinyinya Hill, and the residents are vulnerable to landslides as well as water constraints during prolonged dry spells.

The site is located in Nyamagabe district which has a high population density and over 75% of its land area relies on compacted soil and limited vegetation. The results in low permeability and limited recharge of groundwater, with the risk of flooding and landslides being exacerbated by poor storm water leading to storm run off and erosion especially during the wetter months of April and November.

Erosion from storm water is a significant problem as it undermines the stability of buildings, roads and footpaths with the potential to cause landslides. The Ministry of Infrastructure reports that just 62% of urban areas in Rwanda are covered by drainage systems[5]. In addition, many informal settlements on Kinyinya Hill live in areas covered by master plans which have storm water considerations. High levels of run-off arise due to the concentration of impervious surfaces and poor drainage infrastructure. The result is low permeability and limited recharge of groundwater with the risk of flooding and landslides being exacerbated by poor storm water leading to storm run off and erosion especially during the wetter months of April and November.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Water supply in the community is intermittent.

Ngyapirika Village is connected to the public potable water utility line that comes from a Water Treatment Plant (WTP) managed by Water and Sanitation Fund (WASAF). In order to meet demand, the WTP produces approximately 70,000 GPD and unfurlatable for many residents. Households that lack a connection typically purchase water from vendors at a cost of $1.50 per 20 liters. If there is no water supply or if the demand exceeds the supply and households tend to store water at home during periods when the supply is shut off, it results in an increase in water consumption and water wastage.

Occasionally if there is a longer water shortage, water trucks supply water to the area, but this is too expensive for many residents. The village has no central sewer network system; therefore, most of the households who cannot afford packaged water, but residents report that boiling water takes too long and is expensive due to the need to purchase charcoal or firewood. WASAF is shifting its focus from a centralized water supply management to the supply available for the whole area, and WASAF is shifting to a better water quality management which shall not require boiling potable water before drinking by the time of project implementation.

## Water meter harvesting (not real data) is common throughout the village.

Water used after residents purchase water. A household water meter in the community.


Most people living in Kigali use latrines as there is no central sewer network. The common practice with latrines is that when they become full and to dig a new pit latrine in another location, it is difficult for exhauster trucks to reach all of the intermittent settlements in Kigali, and the cost to emptying is too high for most households. In Ngyapirika Village, only 40% of the households have access to improved sanitation facilities; while only 41% of households have a flush toilet. In Ngyapirika Village, the pit latrine is the most common used but there are also some dry toilets and composting toilets in use.

The village has no central sewer network system for the long term, for example the Kigali Centralized Sewerage System Project (KCSSP) which was launched by the Government of Rwanda in 2013. The KCSSP is a project aimed at providing sanitation services to all households and institutions within the city limits of Kigali. The project will provide sewer connections for the safety of non-sewered users. The organic material in latrine pits may cause emissions of methane or GHG emissions. The use of latrines makes communities vulnerable in cases of water scarcity due to lack of contamination.

Current transport infrastructure is underdeveloped with poor accessibility, reliability and resilience and posing threats to properties through erosion and flooding hazards. Roads are packed clay earth with erosion cracks and channels, and are consistently wide enough for passing vehicles. Adjacent buildings and the slope of the


---
**Page 13**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 9

hil contain road widths for roads across the slope, and some roads are very steep (10-25% gradient). There many hedges along the roadside and some up the hill, and erosion channels are present as well as erosion and flooding controls such as sandbags. There are numerous slip hazards especially in wet conditions, and railings and safe barriers are not present on most of these roads. The road lighting is poor and there is no street or way lighting is present on buildings but is not continuous on thoroughfares. The poor infrastructure and inadequate lighting make it difficult to navigate the area at night. There is a lack of public transport in the lower part of the hill and the motorcycle taxi service which operates throughout Kigali. Conditions for cycling are very poor and conditions for pedestrians are also poor.

# Theory of Change Narrative for Green City Project

## Impact: Paradigm Shift

If technical and financial support is provided to develop a replicable climate-resilient, low-emission upgrade infrastructure, capacity and demand for scale-up will be developed. THEN vulnerable households will have reduced exposure to landslides, flooding, and heat stress along with better coping mechanisms and adaptive capacity. AND the overall energy intensity of buildings, cities, industries, and appliances will be reduced. AND climate risks will be mitigated and pathways established to ensure replication and scale-up across the region.

## Outcomes:

1. **Strengthened Adaptive Capacity**: Heat Stress Mitigation, Reduced Exposure to Climate Risks: Activities will focus on improving infrastructure and services to withstand climate risks such as heat stress, landslides, flooding, and droughts by upgrading public infrastructure and implementing nature-based solutions for stormwater management.

2. **Strengthened Institutional and Regulatory Systems for Climate-Responsive Planning and Development**: The institutional framework will be strengthened to plan, regulate, and implement climate-responsive and low-emission development strategies.

### Co-benefits:
I. Reduced Energy Intensity of Buildings, Cities, Industries, and Appliances
   - Promoting the use of renewable energy and energy-efficient technologies to lower overall energy consumption and carbon footprint of urban areas.
II. Gender Empowerment through Inclusive Planning and Capacity Building
   - Ensuring that women and marginalized groups are included in planning and implementation processes to ensure equitable access to climate-resilient development opportunities.
III. Enhanced Local Employment and Skill Development
   - Creating job opportunities through the construction and maintenance of upgraded infrastructure and services, as well as through vocational training and skill development programs.

### Outputs and Activities:

#### Output 1.1: Resilient Transport, Water, and Energy Infrastructure

- **Activity 1.1.1**: Detail climate-resilient and emissions-reducing engineering design for Nagaynuka Village. Developing comprehensive and integrated engineering designs that incorporate climate resilience into transport, water, and energy infrastructure.
- **Activity 1.1.2**: Implement resilient green transport/nobility models and green construction methods; Establishing a network of green transport options and utilizing sustainable construction materials and methods.
- **Activity 1.1.3**: Implement resilient stormwater management systems using nature-based solutions; Utilizing nature-based solutions such as green ditches and permeable surfaces to manage stormwater and prevent erosion.

B1: Challenges in community participation and behavior change; Difficulty in achieving active and sustained community engagement and changes in behavior necessary for the project's success.
B2: Potential delays in implementation due to regulatory or logistical issues; Regulatory hurdles and logistical complexities that could slow down the progress of project activities.

- Consistent regulatory support and favorable policies for sustainable urban development.

The ToC also demonstrates how specific project activities contribute to broader mitigation and adaptation goals.

This outcome focuses on enhancing the capacity of governments and communities to adapt to climate change by integrating climate considerations into emission reduction strategies. By strengthening regulatory frameworks and institutional capacities for climate-resilient development, this project contributes directly to GCF MRA and ARA.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

The project comprises several outcomes, outputs, and activities identified through a participatory process.

## Outcomes and Outputs

### Outcome 1: Strengthened Adaptive Capacity, Heat Stress Mitigation, and Reduced Exposure to Climate Hazards

#### Output 1.1: Resilient Transport, Water, and Energy Infrastructure

##### Activity 1.1.1: Detailed Climate-Resilient and Emissions-Reducing Engineering Design for Nagayakina Village including transport and mobility, stormwater management, water supply, sanitation, and energy infrastructure.

The activity involves developing comprehensive engineering designs that incorporate climate resilience and emissions reduction concepts:
(i) coverage of high vulnerability areas (e.g., floodplains and landslides with coverage for future projection of climate change for these climate hazards),
(ii) consideration of the impacts on the environment including biodiversity,
(iii) ensuring resilience during construction phase, (iv) analysis of land use and water resources to ensure that infrastructure can withstand the impacts of climate change,
(v) enabling the construction in accordance with the design, (vi) specification of materials and technologies for resilient infrastructure, (vii) consideration of the need for adaptation measures, including stabilization measures where necessary, enabling determination of the use of pre-defined budget, (viii) ensuring that the project is designed to be implemented within the allocated budget,
(ix) effective O&M of the constructed infrastructure, to be financed under Outcome 2.1e), (x) defining O&M responsibilities and procedures for maintenance and repair of the infrastructure, (xi) considering all aspects for the procurement of the construction company/consortium, and the oversight engineering solutions for the project, (xii) ensuring that the design is such that the infrastructure can withstand the impacts of climate change, including increased rainfall and heat stress, (xiii) ensuring that the design is such that it can be implemented by economically vulnerable households (see feasibility study, page 45), and therefore this step is crucial to provide a solid foundation for all subsequent infrastructure development, ensuring it is both climate-proof and sustainable.

##### Activity 1.1.2: Implement Resilient Green Transport/Mobility Networks and Green Construction Methods

The poor quality of rights of way and access due to high levels of run-off and erosion presents significant challenges in the village (see feasibility study, page 45). This activity involves developing a Green Rights of Way Network using green construction methods. The aim is to improve safety and security, which are further risk from increasing climate hazards, and establish resilient infrastructure that will benefit the community to encourage sustainable means of transport and improve resilience and security.

##### Activity 1.1.3: Implement Resilient Storm Water Management Systems Using Nature-Based Solutions

Erosion problems significantly impact property and personal safety. Improved stormwater management is critical for helping the community adapt to increased rainfall events due to climate change, as well as other extreme weather conditions such as flooding, landslides, droughts, swales, gullies, and detention-filtration-percolation areas to prevent erosion by absorbing rainfall runoff and reducing surface water flow. This will also help in maintaining the quality of soil and soil particles together. This activity will include a stormwater conventional system setup if the village is not yet equipped with such systems. The project will also consider best practices implementing rainwater harvesting systems on suitable households and community high-priority areas.

##### Activity 1.1.4: Expand, Install, and Ensure Resilience of Central Water Supply and Rainwater Harvesting Systems


---
**Page 21**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## Activity 1.1.5: Promotion and Implementation of Renewable Energy and Energy-Efficient Technologies

Affordability and low awareness are key barriers to the uptake of low-emission technologies at the household level (Feasibility Study, page 98). This activity involves promoting and implementing solar PV systems, energy-efficient appliances, and other renewable energy technologies. It also includes providing education and technical assistance related to these technologies. Investing in a range of technologies will help reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

### Output 1.2 Improved Waste and Community Management Systems

#### Activity 1.2.1: Implementation of Resilient Community-Based Solid Waste Management Systems

Poor management of solid waste contributes to GHG emissions, pollution, and public health impacts (Feasibility Study, page 75). This activity involves investing in community composting, neighborhood collection points, and other waste reduction measures. It aims to reduce waste generation, source-separation, and reuse. These measures will develop and operationalize value chains for organic waste management.

#### Activity 1.2.2: Establishment of Resilient Community Facilities for Climate-Resilient Development

Developing facilities such as a TVET center, community focal points, and market squares supports sustainable development and promotes community cohesion (Feasibility Study, page 83). This activity involves establishing a TVET center in each district, rehabilitating the market, and developing community spaces to provide training in climate-resilient construction practices, renewable energy technologies, and using a hub for community services and economic development.

#### Activity 1.2.3: Improve Sanitation with Biogas Recovery Systems

Biogas recovery systems are appropriate for the majority of rural households and can be unsustainable. Developing biogas recovery systems will manage waste, provide renewable energy, and reduce methane emissions (Feasibility Study, page 85). This activity involves establishing a TVET center and market area connected to a biogas recovery system, and providing technical assistance for training improvements and higher education.

##### Activity 2.1.1: Capacity Building and Community Engagement

Providing training and capacity building programs focused on climate-resilient construction practices and the use of renewable energy technologies will help communities adopt sustainable climate-resilient practices (Feasibility Study page 110). This includes engineering capacity, enabling effective implementation of low-emission technologies. The training and capacity-building activity involves developing a curriculum that includes the practical application of skills learned, identifying long-term institutional arrangements for training delivery, and procuring training materials in collaboration with the Rwanda TVET Board, and obtaining accreditation for training centers.

##### Activity 2.1.2: Institutional Capacity Development for Government of Rwanda Agencies/Institutions


---
**Page 22**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Strengthening the Institutional Capacity of Government Agencies to Implement and Manage Climate-Resilient Development Strategies

This activity involves identifying needs and developing a capacity-building strategy for government agencies, including conducting a needs assessment and establishing an evaluation process, and developing training content and materials with a learning and development calendar.

### Activity 2.1.3: Strengthening Planning and Regulatory Frameworks to Support an Enabling Environment

Developing and implementing planning codes and regulations that support climate-resilient and low-emission development strategies (Feasibility Study, page 118). This activity involves identifying the gaps in the current regulatory framework for sustainable and affordable housing projects, and developing and implementing a mainstreaming program.

### Activity 2.1.4: Awareness Raising and Citizen Engagement for Climate Action

Conducting awareness campaigns and engaging citizens in climate-responsive planning and development fosters community ownership and support for sustainable practices (Feasibility Study, page 125). This activity involves creating an awareness campaign to promote energy-efficient appliances, waste separation, recycling, and composting through awareness campaigns, and developing and implementing communication tools.

### Output 2.2 Knowledge Management and Development

#### Activity 2.2.1: Blueprinting the Green City Kigali and Developing Planning Codes for the Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) Facilitated by Climate Resilient Knowledge Management Practices

Creating detailed blueprints and planning codes guides sustainable urban development and ensures that all stakeholders are aligned with climate-resilient development strategies (Feasibility Study, page 130). This activity involves developing a platform to store, manage, and disseminate knowledge on climate-resilient development practices. It also includes systematically collecting and documenting lessons learned on an annual basis, and developing and disseminating knowledge management tools.

#### Activity 2.2.2 Establishment of a Knowledge Management System

The establishment of a knowledge management system (KMS) is a critical component of the project, ensuring that all stakeholders are aligned with climate-resilient development strategies. This will serve as a central repository and dissemination platform for lessons learned from climate-resilient development practices, and other relevant information among stakeholders involved in the Green City Kigali initiative and beyond.

#### Activity 2.2.3 Development of Support Program for Recycling Groups

The development of a support program for recycling groups aims to enhance the effectiveness and sustainability of waste management efforts (Feasibility Study, page 140). This activity involves providing technical and organizational support to local recycling groups, enabling them to operate more efficiently and make significant contributions to waste management and environmental conservation efforts in Nyagatare Village.

### Implementation Strategy

GCF will contribute to the project's impact on local economic development where the local community, government, and private sector work together. It is unique in requiring a financially reproducible model suitable for developing countries as it is replicable. It serves as a model for climate-based resilience strengthening that can be scaled up to other parts of Kigali and secondary cities in Rwanda.

FONERIWA will oversee the staff working on the Nganyiru project, including leads for different activities and specialists such as gender and social experts. This ensures that all aspects of the project are managed effectively and that expertise is applied where needed.

### Advisory Committee

A Technical Advisory Committee will provide guidance to both the Steering Committee and the Project Management Unit (PMU). This committee is composed of members from entities affiliated with the Ministry of Environment (MoE), including Green Climate Fund (GCF) representatives, relevant government institutions including WASAC, REG, CoF, RHA, REMA, RTIDA, and the Rwanda Development Board (RDB). The PMU will prepare a list of potential committee members and send it to the relevant institutions, asking them to appoint a focal point who will serve as the institution's representative. The Ministry of Environment (MoE), in its role as the Accredited Entity (AE), will also be represented on the committee.

### Sector Working Groups (SWGs)

To facilitate the uptake and scaling of project interventions, the PMU will set up results that are communicated to relevant stakeholders through Sector Working Groups (SWG). These groups serve as a forum for dialogue that includes development partners supporting the sector, as determined by the Government of Rwanda in consultation with labor. The SWG helps build synergies in policy formulation and implementation. The PMU will regularly report results to the SWGs and provide inputs for continuous improvement of project delivery.

### Planning Coordination Meetings

The Joint Planning Committee (JPC). Planning Coordination Meetings will be held bi-monthly and will be open to the public. These meetings aim to:

- Coordinate and harmonize development decisions across development sectors and stakeholders in accordance with existing plans.
- Assess building permit and real estate development permit applications, preparing technical documents as necessary.
- Monitor development decisions to ensure they align with applicable planning documents for land use and other purposes, including the harmonization of technical aspects and various interests for the benefit of the public.
- Initiate the development of an internal development strategy where no urban planning document exists. A standard template will be developed by the PMU as a reference planning document.
- Assess and evaluate feasibility studies and development proposals responding to public tenders.

### Community Upgrading Committee

A Community Upgrading Committee has been established at the village level in Nganyiru. This committee's main role is to facilitate information sharing and community participation, working closely with the Nganyiru Upgrading Project and other local agencies. The committee will help residents identify their needs and resources and provide information on contracting, construction supervision, and maintenance.

### Flow of Funds

In line with the flow of funds, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) will disburse funds to the Ministry of Environment (MoE), the Accredited Entity (AE) responsible for the project. The MoE will then transfer these funds to FONERIWA, the Executing Entity (EE), which will manage the funds for the Nganyiru project. The EE will ensure that all project activities are implemented in accordance with the approved budget through a competitive procurement process, ensuring transparent and efficient use of the funds.

Reiards is highly vulnerable to climate change effects, and its readiness[1] to combat these effects is moderate. The city has a population of about 357,489 people, with an average age of 26 years old; it is the climate vulnerability[2]. It is the 20th most vulnerable and 94th least ready country. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events in Reiards, which are likely to exacerbate consequences of rapid urbanisation. This trend is expected to intensify over time due to increasing population density and climate change effects on the property of climate-related threats faced by vulnerable urban communities, coupled with the age structure of the population.

Reiards's topography and climatic profile make it prone to localized floods and landslides. 42% of the city is covered in steep slopes, which are highly susceptible to landslides[3]. Reiards's steep slopes are prone to landslides, which are predicted to increase due to the high precipitation on rain[4] ratio. The city has a population density of about 107 people per km²; its drainage system was established by MIDAMAN in 2019 and 74 people died, 22 persons were injured and over 300 houses were damaged due to landslides. Reiards is also highly vulnerable to flooding[5] due to the high and moderate slope susceptibility, high population density, the concentration of urban infrastructure on steep slopes, and poor drainage systems.

Reiards's vulnerability is increased by its informal settlements, which are located in unplanned, informal settlements which puts additional pressure on services and leads to increased levels of poverty. The city has a high proportion of informal settlements (70%) that account for more than 70% of housing[6] as most formal housing is too expensive for the average resident. This also means that residents are more vulnerable to climate change effects in urbanisation.

Rising demand for water is coinciding with periods of water stress due to prolonged dry spells, a low capture and storage capacity, and a high precipitation-on-rain ratio[7]. There is rising demand for water as Reiards's population increases. The city has a high proportion of informal settlements (60%) in its growing cities, irrigation and industry. Poorly managed sanitation systems also pose a risk to the environment and public health. The city has experienced an increase in water use from 2018-2020 further increasing the pressure on water resources as more homes are connected to mains water supply[8]. This is expected to continue due to increased urbanisation, rising temperatures, agriculture increases, industries develop, and the population grows[9]. Residents in informal settlements experience private water constraints during prolonged dry spells. Reiards's energy,

supply is also threatened from the increasing annual variability and prolonged dry spells as hydropower accounts for a significant share of electricity supply to the country.

Inland settlements tend to be very dense with very little open space and often with concentrated population in urban areas. The vulnerability of these communities increases from rising maximum temperatures, more hot days and heat waves are greatest for infants and young children, pregnant women, older adults, people living with chronic conditions, and those with mental health issues. Higher ambient temperatures with lower dew days is also likely to extend the range and activity of some disease vectors such as mosquitoes and ticks. The projected increase in temperature will result in a reduction by 150% by 2050*. Infants and young children vulnerable living in informal settlements are particularly at risk.

Remains remains dependent on international climate finance for the majority of its climate investments. In 2019, the Government of Rwanda (GoR) received $34 million from the Least Developed Countries (LDCs) with a Gross national income (GNI) per capita of US$830—–the highest amount in the region and the second-highest globally after Ethiopia. The country ranked 159 out of 177*. Despite a range of fiscal incentives, attracting private investment in climate projects remains challenging due to high transaction costs, lack of access to finance, limited access to affordable financing, inadequate infrastructure, ambiguous tax rules and a lack of skills in the workforce. Rwanda’s economy contracted by 2% during the first quarter of 2021 compared with the first recession since 1994 due to the COVID-19 (coronavirus) pandemic with gross domestic product (GDP) falling from $35 billion in Q1 2020 to $34.7 billion in Q1 2021, a fall of approximately 0.8 percent before the COVID-19 outbreak. The construction sector contracted by 7.2 percent in the first quarter compared with the previous year.

The poverty headcount is forecast to rise by 5.1 percentage points (more than 550,000 people) in 2021 compared with a pre-COVID-19 estimate of 38.4%. The government’s revenue from taxes (excluding revenues) is increasing public debt which is estimated to have reached nearly 66 percent of GDP in the first quarter of 2021, up from 57% at end of fiscal year 2020/21. This means that Rwanda does not add to its debt burden**. The project is also timely as it can influence investments in Rwanda’s climate resilience and adaptation projects, which are currently estimated to be around $3 billion (GDP on average per year).

There has been strong political commitment and progressive legal and institutional structures in place to support low emission, climate-resilient growth. Climate change has long been recognized at the highest levels of government as a key development challenge. The country’s national adaptation goals as evidenced by national priorities identified in the updated NDC; GoR is an early adopter of climate finance mechanisms such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the Adaptation Fund. Rwanda’s planning and budgeting processes. Rwanda’s progress environment on climate mitigation and adaptation has been recognized for its innovative and inclusive approaches, progressive arrangements to improve the coordination of environment and climate priorities across cross-cutting sectors, and strong institutional capacity. The GoR is a member of several international groups such as the Natural Resources Sector Working Group and the Environment and Climate Change Sub-Group of the GCF Board. Rwanda has also been recognized for its leadership in gender-related issues**. However, tackling climate change remains highly conditional on the availability of resources.

The request for GCF financing is needed to address key barriers to ensure that the country’s rapid urbanization does not lead to a significant increase in greenhouse gas emissions due to increased variability and shifting to a low emission trajectory. GCF support will help to demonstrate the viability of Rwanda’s climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies, which would otherwise be undermined as urbanization accelerates. GCF support will help to catalyze the low emission, climate-resilient growth agenda in Rwanda. The GoR has already demonstrated its readiness for GCF support. GoR will not have adequate resources or capacity to take forward its ambitious plans to combat climate change and achieve its NDC targets without additional financial support from GCF financing are also needed to strengthen GoR’s institutional and regulatory systems for climate- resilient growth, which would otherwise be undermined as urbanization accelerates. The project aims at enhancing the adaptive capacity of vulnerable urban communities and lower the energy intensity of its cities.

## B.6 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will be implemented through a Community Based Management (CBM) framework based on consultation and involvement of the MoE and other relevant government agencies and communities at all levels. The CBM framework is based on the following elements: i) a strong enabling environment with sectoral policies and strategies (NDC, NAP, etc.) and a high level of country ownership and reliance on local capacity; ii) a strong institutional arrangement for project implementation involving the MoE as well as relevant utilities which ensures that key skills and expertise developed during implementation are retained in-country after completion of the project; iii) a dedicated community upgrading committee maintained a regular dialogue between the design team and the communities to ensure that their needs are met. A Community Based Management (CBM) group has been created as well as make information sharing easier. During operation, the CBM group will be responsible for ensuring that the infrastructure is properly maintained with the community and to develop local ambassadors that will be involved in the upgrade process on a regular basis.

All Operation & Maintenance (O&M) arrangements are in place and the local authority and community ownership of the infrastructure has been established. The project will ensure sustainability in the long term. All infrastructure investments will be undertaken in close collaboration with local entities to ensure their full ownership and responsibility for maintenance, operation and management of their homes with service support and training from the suppliers including the provision of operation and maintenance manuals. This will include a five-year warranty period on all major supplier contracts along with the requirement for five-year warranties. These measures will be consolidated within the project design and will ensure that the infrastructure is properly maintained during operation and maintenance of the infrastructure after the project has ended. The table below summarizes the responsibilities of each party in relation to the operation and maintenance.

The assignment of activities and budget for O&M (upgraded climate-resilient and emissions-reducing technologies) will be completed following completion of Activity 1.2.1, further amended when construction is completed following completion of Activity 1.1.2. Project Component

* **Transport / mobility improvements** (Construction of footpaths only; green road construction methods)
    * City of Kigali will be responsible for road construction and maintenance. The contractor will perform some on-going maintenance.
* **Blue/Green stormwater management systems**
    * WASAC will be responsible for maintaining the stormwater management systems. The contractor will perform some limited on-going maintenance.
    * Households will be responsible for maintaining the manholes.
* **Expansion of central water supply (100% connection rate)**
    * WASAC will be responsible for O&M of the system, with funds from the water tariff from users.
* **Rainwater harvesting (as supplement to central supply)**
    * City of Kigali will be responsible for maintaining the systems.
* **Fixtures for Household Water Treatment / Efficient Fixtures**
    * Households will be responsible for maintaining any systems if purchased.

## Bogas system and TVET / market
Operation and maintenance will be managed by the TVET building management. There will be a small fee for public use of the toilets, which will cover operational costs.

Households will be responsible for maintaining the systems as they are implemented.

## Community composting
The responsibility will lie with persons responsible for the community composting.

## Waste and Recycling collection stations
The waste collection company will pick up all recyclable materials. The cost of bags, funded by the collection fees. The community will need to provide the bags.

## Solar PV and water heaters
Maintenance of solar PVs on public roads will be done by the City of Kigali.
Maintenance of the solar PV system in the pilot will be done by a solar PV provider, funded by user-paid tariffs.

Operation and maintenance will be done by the cook stove manufacturer.

### Financial sustainability of the GCF investment is predicated on the strengthening of the enabling environment and the development of investable projects. The strengthening of policy and regulatory frameworks at the national level will create an enabling environment to foster more demand and investments in climate change mitigation and adaptation.
The capacity and awareness building interventions will strengthen institutional capacity for public private partnerships, including through the establishment of a Technical and Vocational Education and Training (TVET) Centre. This will be done with a decreasing level of external support. This will target key Government institutions including MoEF, UNDP, ILO, and others. The TVET Centre will also provide training in climate change adaptation programming sustainable upgrades projects and new climate responsive urban developments.
The centre will be established as an independent institution that will provide technical assistance to government and private sector actors on the development of investment proposals targeting key sectors including the construction industry and recycling industry and providing support for the implementation of these projects. The TVET Centre will also facilitate dialogue with government and communities. Extensive engagement with the private sector during the design and implementation phases is critical in order to ensure that investments are sustainable.
The TVET Centre will play a key role in sustaining investments in capacity development, it will serve as an important platform for knowledge sharing and dissemination of best practices and lessons learned from the GCF portfolio projects. The TVET Centre will be established at the University of Rwanda, Kigali campus. Students will have easy access to completed works demonstrating the viability of investments in renewable energy and climate change adaptation.
The TVET Centre will also provide training on the development of a governance structure and business plan that will ensure 50% representation from women and the establishment of an advisory board and mechanism with at least 30% representation from women. The TVET will be integrated into the existing network of TVETs across the country and supported through the GCF portfolio programme.
Knowledge generated through the development of the GCA pilot and upgrade as well as best practice and lessons learned through the implementation will be shared widely through an information portal

and used to scale-up project interventions across the country. Further the project seeks to develop a project design template for future affordable and resilient housing projects in secondary cities. This will be done through workshops during the development of detail design for the GCK pilot project. The goal is to lower the cost barriers to entry for affordable and resilient housing, while also ensuring that these developments are adaptive and mitigative, and economically affordable developments as opposed to conventional housing solutions which are often not affordable or accessible in secondary cities.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section describes the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Total Investment Framework.

## D.1. Maximum length (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Continued urban development on a business as usual basis (BAU) will lock Rwanda into unsustainable, high carbon pathways with low resilience to climate change. Unsustainable consumption and production patterns and inadequate response capacity to extreme weather events are major challenges for the country.

One third of the reduction in poverty between 2014 and 2017 was due to the movement of people from the rural areas to urban areas, which is expected to continue. However, this trend has been accompanied by a decline in agricultural income into more productive sectors raising wages and skills (urban workers in Rwanda earn, on average, twice the national minimum wage). The project will contribute to poverty reduction through increased access to land for agriculture with an inter-organic approach to apply climate resilient approaches. Higher density walkable cities reduce levels of urban pollution and greenhouse gas emissions as well as reducing road traffic accidents. This will result in reduced CO2 emissions and air pollution as well as reduced development of housing on steep slopes which are vulnerable to flooding.

### Adaptation Impact Potential:

The proposed GCF project contributes to increased climate-resilient development in the following ways:

* **GCF adaptation results area A10:** Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and households. The project will contribute to improved access to water supply systems, especially during rain events. This is a major risk for the project community. The proposed permeable road construction materials, as well as the use of green roofs and walls in buildings, will reduce runoff from urban areas. This will reduce flooding on streets, roads, other infrastructure, resulting in $4M USD in economic assets protected from the impact of extreme climate events.

* **GCF adaptation results area A12:** Increased health and well-being, and food and water security; increasing community capacity and quality, including disaster risk management. The project will contribute to improved access to clean drinking water and sanitation, increased access to disaster risk reduction services, and improved urban planning. Improved land use zoning and the centralisation of the water supply system will result in a round-access to reliable and safe water supplies.

* **GCF adaptation results area A13:** Increased resilience of infrastructure and the built environment to climate change impacts. The project will contribute to increased access to clean drinking water, sanitation, and energy services which will result in damage to houses, roads and other infrastructures, water shortages, and power cuts. The proposed permeable road construction materials, as well as the use of green roofs and walls in buildings, will reduce runoff from urban areas.

* **GCF outcomes Area M7:** Lower energy intensity of buildings, cities, industries and appliances. The project will result in 13 institutional and regulatory system improvements, including the development of a climate change adaptation strategy, and 735 physical structures (based on number of households, including annexes buildings in community compounds).

### GCF Outcomes Area M7:

Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-resilient planning and development. The project will contribute to the implementation of the Rwanda Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan, emission development of urban infrastructure and buildings. It will also budget the GCF and planning code for the City of Kigali, which will be used as a model for other cities in Rwanda. Future green city developments.

The project will result in three institutional and regulatory system improvements:

* **GCF Outcome M7.1:** Strengthened climate change adaptation strategy. The project will contribute to strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks for 30,252 beneficiaries through the implementation of a climate change adaptation strategy.
* **GCF Outcome M7.2:** Low emission sustainable development pathways. The project will contribute to low-emission sustainable development pathways. This includes the installation of solar street lights instead of diesel generators in urban areas and the installation of solar panels on roofs of buildings, industries and appliances.
* **GCF outcome M7.3:** Reduced emissions from buildings, cities, industries and appliances. The project will result in a reduction of 102,548 tonnes CO2e/year through the implementation of energy efficient technologies such as LED street lights, solar panels on roofs of buildings, industries and appliances, and TVET.

The TVET will also have an operation savings of $9k CO2e/year due to the green measures installed. The project will contribute to a reduction in urban heat island effect by 10% through the installation of permeable road construction materials, as well as the use of green roofs and walls in buildings.

### GCF Outcome M7.6:

Lower energy intensity of buildings, cities, industries and appliances. The TVET will decrease building power energy intensity in buildings through the efficient design proposed (estimated to save less than 2,100 kWh/month).

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Rwanda's capacity to adapt to climate change is low. Rwanda ranks 131 out of 187 countries in the 2016 GCI, which measures a country’s ability to manage its natural resources and reduce vulnerability to climate change effects, yet it readies itself to combat these effects is moderate. Due to its dense river network and large water bodies, Rwanda has been able to mitigate some of the impacts of climate change on water availability and weather related hazards. The impacts of climate change in Rwanda are exacerbated by a range of anthropogenic factors such as deforestation, urbanization, and population growth. These factors have led to increased pressure on agriculture by the majority of the population.

As shown in Table D.1, households for household water availability and food security their burdens are expected to increase disproportionately due to climate change*. The future economic cost of climate change was estimated at 2% of GDP per year between 2035-2049. Women bear a disproportionate burden of poverty (with 16% of the population living in extreme poverty), with women representing a higher 39.5% poverty level than men.

Rwanda's high rate of urbanisation increases its overall vulnerability to climate change. Urban households are more vulnerable because they have limited access to water and sanitation services, poor drainage systems, informal settlements, infiltration rates for storm water are low creating high runoff speeds leading to erosion and downstream flooding. The number of people living in urban areas has increased from 10% in 2005 to 34% in 2016. Urban water considerations was mentioned in 2016*. Moreover, low recharge rates for groundwater tables reduce the availability of water resources in rural areas. This is further exacerbated by high population growth and urbanisation. The increase in rural-urban migration has resulted in widespread development of slums (particularly in Kigali which is the most populous city). These slums are characterized by poor infrastructure, lack of access to basic services, and high density housing less able to withstand extreme weather events and a lack of risk reducing infrastructure. Homes, possessions and livestock are often built on unstable land with little or no drainage systems. The proportion of households in informal settlements account for more than 70% of housing* as most formal housing is too expensive for the average resident. Informal settlements are characterized by poor sanitation facilities, high population density, lack of access to water and electricity. These slums are often located on floodplains and are prone to flooding during heavy rains and days and heat waves are greater for infants and young children, the elderly, expectant mothers, and those with health problems. The informal settlements also have poor drainage systems which can lead to the spread of vector-borne disease vectors including mosquito and tick-borne diseases with population at risk from malaria increasing by 150% since 2006.

42%. Of the country's area is classified as having a moderate to very high susceptibility to landslides. Rwanda’s terrain is mountainous, which increases its vulnerability to landslides. The number of people living in areas with climate change. 43% of Rwanda’s health facilities** 25% of schools and 74% of district roads are exposed to landslides. In the period between 1980-2016, there were over 172,000 people affected by landslides in Rwanda. Of these, 623 persons were injured and out of 52,000 houses damaged or destroyed.

44%. The country has a high vulnerability to water scarcity due to its low rainfall (800 mm per year), dry prolonged dry spells, a low capture and storage capacity, and a high precipitation run-off rate*. There is a rising demand for water in the context of per capita water availability. This is further exacerbated by urbanization and population growth. The country’s Water demand is therefore expected to increase rapidly by 2040 further increasing the pressure on water resources as

The risk of landslides for housing in Kigali City is especially high due to the high and moderate slope terrain, urbanization, population density, the conservation of informal settlements, and use of low-quality construction materials.

The following are identified as key challenges: (1) poor road network; (2) uncoordinated transport services; (4) inadequate water and waste management systems; 5) limited access to basic social services such as education, health care, and sanitation; 6) high levels of youth productivity and unemployment.

Limited financial resources are available to address these challenges. Existing programs and interventions are insufficient to adequately address the challenges described above. In addition, there is a significant risk that climate change pressures on already stretched government spending. This project cannot be implemented without GCF support.


## Existing national climate strategies

Most of the country's climate-related policy milestones and strategic frameworks have been significantly informed by vulnerability assessments and adaptation planning.

Baseline National Vulnerability Index conducted in 2015 and follow up provincial and District vulnerability index of 2016. The study aimed to provide a report on a new District vulnerability assessment and comparative Index for the 30 districts of Rwanda. The study was commissioned by the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Disaster Management (MECCDM) in 2015 and other relevant national and international reports and publications.

The 2019 study aimed to provide a report on a new District vulnerability assessment and comparative Index for the 30 districts of Rwanda. The study was commissioned by the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Disaster Management (MECCDM) in 2015 and other relevant national and international reports and publications.

Policy and strategy recommendations to address climate change have been embedded into Rwanda's National Development Strategy (NDS).

Notably in 2022, the World Bank produced a Country Climate Development Report (World Bank, 2022) for Rwanda which provides an overview of the country’s progress on climate action. The report highlights that Rwanda has made significant progress towards its NDCs and other climate-related commitments.

The analysis have consequently been embedded in Rwanda's national development frameworks. These include:

- The National Strategy for Transformation (NST 2018) which sets out a vision to transform Rwanda into an upper-middle-income country by 2035 and high-income status by 2050.
- NST-2018 (2019) has identified accessible Sustainable Urbanisation' is a key priority which includes the provision of adequate infrastructure in urban areas, including roads, water supply, sanitation, electricity, and other essential infrastructure for 200,000 new residential zones. The Urbanisation and Rural Settlement Sector Strategic Plan (URSSP) was launched in 2019 to guide this process.
- Other key strategic orientation for urbanization include, but are not limited to: the Green Growth and Climate Resilience Strategy (GGCRS), which is a national strategy that aims to transform Rwanda into an upper-middle-income country by 2035; the National Environment and Climate Change Policy enacted in 2019.

Rwanda has demonstrated capacity to implement a GCF-funded project and consistently delivers results against targeted indicators. The Ministry of Energy (MoE) has also established many institutional frameworks and networks under which energy MoE has a reliable track record for implementing climate change projects. For example, MoE is implementing the Rwanda Solar Home System Project (RSHS), which aims to provide access to clean energy in rural areas.

Alignment with existing policies

Rwanda has committed itself to submit a tougher target than the UN, within which a target of 35% of its emissions and an at least 16% (unconditionally) by 2030 compared with a business-as-usual scenario. The country is also committed to achieving 40% renewable energy in total electricity generation capacity by 2030 through the deployment of hydro and solar energy, improved energy efficiency in industrial processes, introduction of vehicle emission standards, cutting out electric vehicles and promoting on-farm biogas use.

Rwanda's urban jurisdictions, the City of Kigali and the secondary cities have recently developed master plans for urban development.
