# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 34

Research and policy actions in the NDC draw upon the Green Growth and Climate Resilience Strategy (GGCRS) [1]. The GGCRS is central to the achievement of the government key development target of becoming a developed climate-resilient nation by 2050. It provides a framework for achieving this goal through promoting sustainable urbanisation and the promotion of green innovation in industrial and private sectors; integrated approach to land use planning, including the protection of natural resources; and the integration of climate change adaptation into development plans. The GGCRS will help to deliver against key programmes of action in the GGCRS including supporting integrated approaches to land use planning and sustainable urbanisation, promoting green innovation in industrial and private sectors, diversifying energy sources with promoting green technology, green industry and private sector development; developing a national climate change adaptation strategy and building capacity for climate change adaptation.

It is noted that full implementation of the GGCRS[2][3][4][5], the NDC and other strategic developmental areas in the country will require significant investments in infrastructure as well as in the form of finance, capacity building and technology transfer to which this proposal has the potential to contribute.

### Planned Interventions

Planned interventions will benefit from the existence of strong policy and institutional frameworks to address climate change. The NDC is supported by a number of enabling policies and strategies including the National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) for the development and implementation of progressive institutional arrangements, policies and strategies for addressing climate change. The NDC also builds on the existing institutional framework that has been in place since 2013 when there are active climate champions across national government. The MEF (A) and FORNERIA (EE) have experience developing and implementing a number of large-scale projects focused on climate change adaptation and mitigation, successfully managing other large programs focused on climate change mitigation and adaptation. As the national climate change focal point, FORNERIA has been at the forefront of climate change policy development in the country with a significant portfolio of climate finance and is therefore well equipped to implement the project. FORNERIA channels financial resources from various sources including the World Bank, European Union, and other donors for successful interventions through a proven and capable national institution such as FORNERIA with high levels of accountability and transparency.

### Relevant National Development Authority

REMA (the NDA) has been actively engaged during the preparation of this proposal and will continue to be involved with discussions on the project and proposed mainstreaming activities. FORNERIA routinely works closely with NDA and particularly had key role in going consultations with REMA’s climate change unit, responsible for preparing national communication and other related documents. The NDA has been a strong partner in the preparation of this proposal and instrumental in supporting the analysis of climate threats and vulnerability baseline included in this proposal. As such, FORNERIA will continue to work closely with the NDA during implementation of the project.

### Engagement with Civil Society Organizations

Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, women and others is a strong emphasis on ensuring the participation of multiple stakeholders in the project including the local authority, community groups, private sector actors, and other key stakeholders. The NDC has been developed through several rounds of public consultation to ensure the interventions maximised benefits for local people that benefited from sustainable urbanisation and green innovation. The GGCRS also provides a framework for ensuring that planning and development at the local level takes account of climate change during and beyond programme activities.

### Country Ownership

Country ownership is rooted in high-level Government commitment to delivering Carbon neutral and climate resilience targets set out in the NDC. The GGCRS has been developed through several rounds of public consultation, which was a strong emphasis on ensuring the participation of multiple stakeholders in the project including the local authority, community groups, private sector actors, and other key stakeholders.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The GGCRS provides a framework for achieving this goal through promoting sustainable urbanisation and the promotion of green innovation in industrial and private sectors; integrated approach to land use planning, including the protection of natural resources; and the integration of climate change adaptation into development plans. The GGCRS will help to deliver against key programmes of action in the GGCRS including supporting integrated approaches to land use planning and sustainable urbanisation, promoting green innovation in industrial and private sectors, diversifying energy sources with promoting green technology, green industry and private sector development; developing a national climate change adaptation strategy and building capacity for climate change adaptation.

### Overview of the financial costs and benefits of the project

Investing in the upgrade of a village to form a climate-responsive blueprint for upscaling and replication is a significant departure from a conventional financial model. Typically speaking, any significant infrastructure upgrades would be


---
**Page 39**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 35

planned and delivered by local government across a 40-60 year horizon or - as in the case of significant road infrastructure - financed through a stand-alone project through user fees, general taxation or government borrowing.

Infrastructure upgrades are typically accompanied by increases in property value with follow-on increases in tax income which can be used to finance further improvements. The proposed climate change adaptation measures will benefit local occupants from homes they can no longer afford, who will then create new informal settlements elsewhere.

The proposed project for climate-responsive upgrades is therefore not only important in creating a scalable, replicable model of adaptation that can be applied across the region but also in providing an opportunity to address the existing poverty and inequality. The additional social and economic measures for the project have been proposed on this basis by providing long-term benefits to all local residents.

From a financial perspective, the project is expected to deliver significant short- and medium-term improvements in the health and wealth for local households, minimising detrimental impacts on local residents from climate change. The cost of implementing these interventions was modest at scale benefiting the village as a whole outside of individually impacted households. The project would be delivered through a combination of public and private sector funding with the majority funded by government and financed through tax revenue. Due to data limitations and because the upgrade would be financed by FYF, it is not possible to quantify the benefits in terms of wealth or income.

In terms of replicability and scalability, any departure from the subsidy-based model should be accompanied by a detailed assessment of distributional impacts to ensure that existing households are not "priced out" of their current homes due to the cost of infrastructure improvements.

## Overview of economic evaluation

Separate from a financial evaluation which only considers direct financial impacts from any proposed project or intervention, there is also an important need for an economic evaluation in terms of its impact on local residents at the local perspective and a regional perspective. Where these costs and benefits cannot be quantified, they are still relevant to the decision-making process as they can inform policy makers about the distributional impacts of adaptation measures for the project directly affecting a subset of households benefiting from the project. These have been considered in this analysis but are not included in the financial evaluation which only considers total wealth in parallel with the expected increases in local property value, reducing the risk of financially-induced eviction and migration.

Quantified benefits have initially focused on the following mitigation measures:

- Water harvesting on existing homes - additional hours available for working and savings from reduction in pesticide use.
- Savings from generation of power by solar PV
- Savings from use of solar water heating
- Carbon savings
- Savings expected from the change in land use

The impact assessment to local quality of life, could not be quantified at the present time so is not included in this analysis. Additionally, adaptation measures at a social level could not be quantified at this time due to a lack of data on quantification and monetisation of the specific planned interventions, e.g., those improving transport and waste activities.

### E.7 Implementing, reporting and monitoring arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The project will utilize and adhere to FONERWA's established operating framework and M&E procedures that have been used for other development funds such as DFID, KIWU and others for GCF projects. Detailed workplans are developed under a logical framework structure within which activities are linked to deliver outputs and outcomes. The project will be monitored through the use of indicators and progress against these indicators will be reported in quarterly reports of progress both against process and performance indicators and will be used to identify areas for specific management attention and will result in revision

## To annual planning plans and where necessary in year plans and budgets (see dashboard below). All data gathered (household survey) will be difficult to disaggregate by sex and age, as well as other variables such as disability and poverty, including in baseline surveys to ensure a meaningful assessment of inclusive project implementation. The M&E partners will be responsible for reporting against the specific indicators under the oversight of the dedicated PMO supported by the FORNERIA PM. The M&E partners will also contribute to learning and review of appropriate methodologies to optimize performance and results.

The following positions are planned for the project:
- An internationally experienced M&E specialist to develop detailed M&E systems, develop detailed operational guidelines, assess progress and work with stakeholders to recommend corrective actions for "at risk" components where necessary and provide capacity building.
- An M&E officer, based in the Gasa district Centre in Kigali at the point of service provision to undertake data collection and first level quality assurance.

The M&E officer will conduct regular field visits to validate reports from construction, waste management and solar heating / PV installation companies.

Regarding the detailed technical engineering aspects related to the project deliverables, FORNERIA will share with GCF the following documents:
- Detailed design of specific components
- Independent engineering report post-construction as specified in Activity 1.1.2
- Project completion report
- O&M operations manual, including budget appropriation, engineering capacity (covering human resources, access to materials and technology), procurement plan.

FORNERIA has the overall responsibility for monitoring and evaluation of the project. FORNERIA will report to the GCF:
- Annual performance reports, including financial management reports, which will include dates and amounts dispensed for each funded activity and compliance with financial covenants.
- At mid-term review and a final evaluation report. The evaluations will assess the performance of the project against expected results and outcomes. These results will evaluate all cover activities but has not been repeated for each performance indicator in the elaborated M&E plan as in Annex 11.
- On the basis of these reports, FORNERIA will provide an annual progress report to GCF on the status of the project, including a report on a gender.

Data collection management and reporting system. A fit-for-purpose data collection, management and reporting system will be deployed based on existing routines and practices within FORNERIA. The PMU will collect and collate data at an annual and bi-annual basis throughout the project life time. The annual reports will provide details of progress against the project deliverables and the M&E officer will use a dashboard to indicate specific activities, unexpected successes or failures, delays and other relevant information as in table below to be included on the dashboard. This will help the PMU to prioritize efforts.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

#### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** High
- **Impact:** High

#### Description:
The ongoing COVID-19 pandemic continues to affect movement (including travel) and supply chains that delay implementation of the project and result in increased costs.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):
COVID-19 is a black swan event that is impossible to predict. It is hoped that overall increased recognition against the disease will lead to better preparedness for future pandemics. However, nothing can be taken for granted and future lockdowns that affect movement of EE personnel, consultants and construction crews are possible. These risks include delays in project implementation due to travel restrictions, reduced availability of construction materials availability and/or cost. Management of these risks are proposed through the careful monitoring of the situation by the PMU and the contractor. The PMU will also monitor the impact on costs and any potential impact the overall implementation schedule. While it is not currently anticipated, similar extensions to those granted for the COVID-19 pandemic may be required in future.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

#### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** High

#### Description:
Unexpected site, especially sub-surface conditions are encountered on site. It is important that geotechnical and hydrogeological investigations have been carried out prior to commencement of detail design so as much any unforeseen site conditions as possible (topographical surveysing has already been completed for the site as part of the initial feasibility study). Any changes in design during the construction phase by contractors, and PMU will be required, and design changes made as required.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):
The proposed design concept relies upon the use of sustainably produced and locally sourced building materials. However, Rwanda has a limited green building materials industry. The issue of inadequate local supply has been a concern in previous projects. To mitigate this risk, the project should be carefully undertaken in conjunction with local material producers to ensure that sufficient quantities are available for construction. In addition, it is proposed that action timesframes should allow for longer lead times that might be required for a more BAU construction project.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

#### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Medium

#### Description:
Materials unavailability

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 54

## Selected Risk Factor #8
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Low |

### Description
Availability of the affordable housing at the GCK 10ha pilot for install of solar PV and SWH systems.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
The construction timetable for the affordable housing units is anticipated to run in parallel with this GCF programme. It is possible that if the later phases of the construction of the affordable homes is delayed, there will not be sufficient time for the installation of solar PV and SWH systems on these homes before the end of 2017. In such cases households will be able to enter into the contract prior to the 2017 end date. Arrangements should be considered to have a buffer in place for these homes or as an extension of the timeline should there be delays that inhibit solar PV and SWH install.

## Selected Risk Factor #9
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Avoid damage to private property during construction of bulk infrastructure.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Learning from the World Bank financed Rwanda Urban Development Project (RUDP II), a number of unspecified damages was caused to homes during road construction and especially when compacting and excavating for the site. The project team worked with the Ministry of Public Works to develop a mitigation plan that would reduce reasonable risk/will. With the knowledge that this was an issue at all sites with homes of similar socio-economic backgrounds, it is important that the GCF project has a mitigation plan in place to address this issue. This will ultimately be something that can be anticipated for this work and will be a requirement that a plan is incorporated into their work to mitigate this risk.

Since the 1990s Rwanda has set a social foundation for anti-corruption efforts by establishing policies and procedures, strengthening institutions, and implementing specific measures to reduce corruption in public procurement. Specifically, a reduction in administrative corruption is of note for a large project such as the one proposed here. The GCF project will be required to implement a number of anti-corruption measures including campaigns for individuals to report corruption has created a social norm for instance. As a result, Rwanda moved from being ranked 102 out of 143 countries in terms of perceived levels of corruption in 2005 to ranking 68th in the world by 2017.

The International Monetary Fund published an assessment of Anti Money Laundering and Combating Financing of Terrorism (AML/CFT) in Rwanda. The GCF project will be required to implement a number of measures within its system in place to address the issue. At present, the GCF has robust measures to light money laundering and terrorist financing risks. These include but are not limited to: ensuring that all payments are made through a special account; ensuring that all transactions are recorded and reported to the Rwanda Revenue Authority for anti-money laundering, combining the financing of terrorism. The Rwandan government has also put in place a special unit within the Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning (MFE) to monitor and ensure compliance with financial system integrity by conducting effective financial intelligence to counter money laundering, the financing of terrorism and other related crimes. This is done through the implementation of a comprehensive supervision of the Ministry of Finance and economic planning, MfE and the GCF Project under its supervision will also be required to implement these measures.

The UN Security Council Consolidated Sanctions List indicates approximately 20 individuals with ties to Rwanda, a number of whom are involved in money laundering. The GCF project will be required to ensure that all staff checked when hiring for the project and once project sites are selected to ensure these individuals will not be working on the project. This will be done through a background check process which includes a self-report and remedy any risks that may occur, the MfE will maintain a Project level grievance redress mechanism to which any concerns can be reported. Any staff found to have ties with the UN Security Council Sanctions List or prohibited activities will be referred to the appropriate authorities in Rwanda. Assuming all staff will be made

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The project is subject to GCF Safeguards because of the limited number of possible adverse impacts, the site-specific nature of these possible impacts, and that these potential impacts can be avoided or mitigated through the implementation of appropriate safeguards.

### Environmental and Social Safeguard Policies

Seven Ten Policy Safeguards were triggered: PS1 Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts; PS2 Indigenous Peoples; PS3 Biodiversity Conservation; PS4 Community health, safety and security and PS5 Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. PS6: Cultural Heritage.

### Potential positive and negative impacts that will affect the existing environmental in the village direct and area of influence have been analyzed in the EIA report (Chapter 7). The proposed interventions such as rainwater harvesting, water supply system, stormwater management, and sanitation facilities will not cause any adverse effects on the environment. However, the project will have some positive impacts on the island. Stormwater management will substantially improve the sanitation challenges affecting water quality water quantity and health in the community.

### Community displacement and resettlement

Assessment of the project sites indicates that implementation through the right of way (ROW) will affect certain households and associated economic activity in the area. These impacts are anticipated to be temporary, with no permanent adverse effects on the environment or human health. The ROW will not affect all affected individuals and properties, encompassing residences, land, crops, and other pertinent assets. It includes a compensation mechanism that outlines a livelihood restoration plan, and elucidates the mechanisms for its implementation.

### Fulfillment of the action items included in the attached E&S Management Plan (ESMP), adherence to the national laws and regulations, and the project’s commitment to the planning process and documentation for the Project and gaps between these and GCF’s requirements. Where necessary, corrective measures, intended to address any identified risks or impacts, will be developed as part of the ESMP.

### The project has been designed to achieve international standards on general occupational health safety for workers involved in construction activities. The project is expected to be designed and operated in accordance with the GCF’s policy on environmental and social safeguards (ESG) and its commitment to program EDGE of IFC among other standards.

### A cost-benefit analysis will be conducted based on a cost-risk, and an impact-based approach to protecting people, the environment, and public resources with a strong emphasis on risk management and avoidance. The project is expected to provide for voluntary resettlement or compensation for involuntary resettlement to avoid social disruption and minimize costs. In the event that any households and businesses are unable to be relocated voluntarily, they will be offered alternative accommodation.

### In planning phase:

- Community members will be solicited during pre-construction, construction and post-construction to discuss their fears, expectations and suggestions about how subsequent phases of upgrading can be improved;
- Land acquisition and resettlement will comply with the provisions of the land acquisition law no. 32/014 of 11/05/66 relating to Expression in the Public interest article 3 stipulates that only Government entities have the right to acquire land for public use, and the District is required to follow the resettlement guidelines in the high-level RPP;
- Individuals will be expected to find acceptable accommodation preferably in the neighborhood.
- A notice period of at least 3 months to find alternative accommodation for both property owners and tenants shall not exceed the time frame set by the District. The District will communicate with the community on this program and not let the resident community themselves.
- For proposed励志 by resident communities, tenant households will be provided with compensation in accordance with the resettlement guidelines.
- Business owners may continue to operate and their employees should expect to continue to work, but the District will be in communication with them to ensure that by the time construction begins, they have relocated to another site.
- A clear schedule of construction works shall be posted at the sector and cell offices and shared with the community on a weekly basis.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 57

## During construction:
- **Businesses:** The operations of which will be disturbed by construction works, have to relocate to a temporary site.
- **Local authority:** Will closely monitor the works by the Contractor if applicable and ensure minimum disturbances and maximum safety for the public.
- **A specialized Grievance Redress Committee will be formed** for the project's purpose, aiming to support advocacy efforts and promptly address any community-related concerns.

## After construction:
- Continued monitoring of the project impact shall be performed in order to make any adjustment of the National greenhouse gas inventory.
- Continued community consultation.

### Resettlement
- **Involuntary resettlement:** Will be minimized but may be required to install measures to increase access to water and sanitation. The resettlement will be managed in a way that affected households jointly agree on a minimum notice and effect period; if not agreed, 3-month notice period shall be seen reasonable.
- A detailed compensation framework, livelihood restoration plan, Grievance Redress Mechanism and Resettlement Implementation Plan will be developed.

### Indigenous Peoples
- The project area is indigenous People will not be triggered by this project. As per the 2003 post genocide constitution, revised in 2015, demonstrated that there is no indigenous peoples in Rwanda and all forms of discrimination are prohibited.
- Decisions on land use and development projects in the project area are to pay particular attention to the needs of vulnerable groups, landless, elderly, women and children.

### Gender
- With that said, taking into consideration the scope of application of the CGI Indigenous Peoples Policy, this is considered a gender sensitive project. The CGI Indigenous Peoples Policy will be applied.
- **PPT only becomes relevant in Rwanda for transitory projects**.

## Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In line with the principles and rationale of the CGI Gender Policy, a gender assessment and gender action plan have been conducted as part of the proposal for the Ngayinya Village Upgrade project. The gender assessment and GAP were conducted in two phases: Phase I was completed in June 2016; Phase II was completed in August 2017.
- **The findings from the Gender Assessment:** Will be used to develop a gender action plan (GAP) that will address the identified gaps and barriers. The assessment was conducted with men and women community members of various ages and backgrounds living in the target project area.

### Key dimensions of the Gender Action Plan
1. **Dimension 1: Participation and Leadership:** This dimension addresses specific gaps and inequalities in the current decision-making structures and processes that are preventing women from participating fully in the upgrade, as well as village governance more broadly and its impact on future responses to climate change events and consideration of the social and environmental gains made during the upgrade process.
2. **Dimension 2: Safety:** This dimension addresses the gender barriers women and girls currently face at the home and community level that are preventing them from participating in decision-making processes related to the upgrade, as well as having the time and agency to be able to participate in decision making processes related to the upgrade.
3. **Dimension 3: Wrap-around Stakeholder Engagement for Women's Equal Access and Benefit:** This dimension addresses gender issues across some of the larger surrounding structures, systems and key

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

## Financial Management and Procurement (max: 500 words, approximately /1 page)

### Financial Management

The GCF has robust public financial management systems in place, and these will be applied to ensure the effective use of resources for this project. The project will follow GCF procedures for planning, budget, and accounting, as outlined in the following:

- Circular Law no: 12/2013/34 of 12/2013 on state finance and property (Official Gazette No° Special of 05/11/2013);
- Memorandum of Understanding No. 165 of 28/01/2016 relating to financial reporting (Official Gazette No° Special of 03/05/2016);
- Supporting operational manuals issued by the Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning (MINECOFIN).

MINECOFIN has established a Multi-Fund Enterprise Management System (MFEMS) for GCF projects, which means that funds received by the MCF are "on budget" from the Government's point of view and will be included in the MCF's annual financial statements. The MFEMS is based on the International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS) related to modified cash accounting principles issued by the MINECOFIN.

The MCF and FONERWA are the primary entities receiving and managing project funds. They will use the Government Financial Management System (GFMS), which includes the Integrated Financial Management Information System (IFMIS) which forms key internal operations. The budget classification used sets out the structure of the GFMS, which is based on the International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS) related to modified cash accounting principles issued by the MINECOFIN.

The tendering process will include assessment of the capacity of their financial management. No bonds will be made under this project and no guarantees will be required from any party. The GCF will ensure that there is an appointment of a number of key posts to ensure that fiduciary risk is identified and managed, including:

- A Financial Officer (FO) who will be responsible for the overall financial accounting and reporting arrangements;
- An internal auditor (based in MCF);
- A Project Manager (PM) who will be responsible for project management.

All supporting financial records will be properly filed and retained to facilitate internal control systems, and the project will ensure that all financial information is accurate. The GCF will issue a Financial Reporting to MCF; this will take place quarterly through key internal controls such as production of expenditure reports and other relevant documents. These reports will be presented to the Project Steering Committee every three months.

### Internal and External Audits

The GCF will ensure that all projects are in accordance with Act 50 of mid-2018 onwards. This will be a full time post funded through the AE Fund. This person will supplement MCF's and FONERWA's internal audit capacity, with a mandate to assess the integrity of project implementation and financial management systems. The GCF will ensure that all projects are subject to annual audit, which will be implemented through a compliance based annual Internal Audit (IA) workplan. IA reports will be submitted by the end of each year and will be audited annually by independent external auditors in consultation with the Office of the Auditor General.

Procurements will be conducted under the regulatory regime in Rwanda which applies to all Government entities including GCF projects.
- Law no 62/2018 of 25/08/2018 on public procurement as amended to date:

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 59

F

**Minimum requirements no 7/2020/10/TC of 19/05/2020 establishing regulations on public procurement, standard bidding documents and standard contracts as amended to date.**

Embedded within these regulations are fundamental principles such as fairness, transparency, accountability, and the prohibition of favoritism or undue influence. These principles aim at reducing the risk of market distortions by fostering a level playing field for all participants. Transparent procedures, including open bidding processes, are designed to ensure that all parties have equal access to information and opportunities on an equal proposition—rather than favoritism or undue influence. Furthermore, the promotion of competition within the procurement process is essential in order to secure the best value for money when procuring goods and services at competitive prices. This not only benefits the project by maximizing value for money but also contributes towards a more efficient use of public resources and fosters a culture of integrity and transparency.

For further insights into the procurement plan and specific strategies employed to ensure adherence to regulatory principles, stakeholders are encouraged to refer to Annex III.

Dear Madam,

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Ministry of Environment regarding the Green City Kigali Project.

We refer to the project titled "The Green City Kigali Project" in Rwanda as included in the funding proposal submitted by The Ministry of Environment to us on 9 July 2024.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Rwanda Environment Management Authority, the National Designated Authority of Rwanda.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Rwanda has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, environmental and social safeguards.
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

2.1 Project background

Rwanda is among the most climate-vulnerable countries, already experiencing significant impacts of climate change. These impacts include increased incidence of drought, flooding and landslides. Climate variability has already led to heavy rainfall events causing infrastructure damage, loss of livelihoods and lives. The challenges faced by Rwandan communities are exacerbated by a lack of access to resilient infrastructure, inadequate or inadequate climate risk-reducing infrastructure and limited capacity to manage climate risks. Upgrading existing infrastructure and building new climate-resilient infrastructure is essential for securing the well-being of the population and supporting sustainable development.

The proposed project aims to address these climate challenges through a comprehensive approach that includes strengthening community resilience, building climate-resilient modernized infrastructure and improving access to low-carbon decentralized basic services and building capacity. The project will be implemented in 10 sites across Rwanda using an integrated model for climate-resilient urban development in Rwanda and the region. The project presents strong global environmental benefits and significant co-benefits for over 30,000 men and women among the most vulnerable populations.

The project has strong climate benefits, as well as social, economic, health and environmental co-benefits. It is estimated that the project will reduce total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 125,032 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2 eq) over the lifetime of the investment. The project also has significant co-benefits for over 487,690 direct and 1,215,000 indirect beneficiaries. The beneficiaries will gain from comprehensive resilience building interventions and enhanced capacity to manage climate risks.

The project is being funded by a grant requested to provide a grant of USD 27.9 million and grant co-financing of USD 7.3 million has been secured from the Government of Rwanda. The project is subject to environmental and social safeguards category B and is set to be implemented over 5 years, with a total lifespan of 60 years.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Outcome 1: Strengthened Adaptive Capacity, Heat Stress Mitigation, and Reduced Exposure to Climate Risks (total cost USD 28.4 million; GCF cost: USD 27.9 million)

Outcome 1 focuses on strengthening the adaptive capacity of the community by comprehensively addressing climate risks through improved water management, infrastructure, and improving community access to decentralized resilient and low-carbon basic services and community to enhance resilience to climate risks such as flooding, landslides, and heat stress. The project will be implemented in 5 sites across Rwanda using integrated urban planning and mobility, implementing stormwater management systems using green, permeable networks and rainwater harvesting.

Moreover, the project addresses sanitation by installing biogas toilets and workers providing sanitation training, along with promoting low-emission technologies such as solar photovoltaic (PV) water pumps. The project will also improve waste management through community composting and recycling centres, and communities will be trained in waste segregation and recycling to reduce litter on streets and open spaces.

The project ensures active community involvement through an inclusive sustainability committee that will oversee the implementation of the project, ensure proper waste separation and recycling. The TVET centre will provide vocational training for climate-responsive upgrades, fostering a skilled workforce.

3.1 Impact potential

The project demonstrates significant potential to contribute to SDG objectives in adaptation and mitigation with co-benefits. The project comprehensively enhances climate resilience of 30,252 men and women in vulnerable and poor informal settlements. Further, the project will contribute to sustainable development in Rwanda, improve access to clean water and sanitation for 148,769 people, and enhance health and well-being of the residents, potentially positively impacting further 1,332,686 indirect beneficiaries. The project also has significant potential to reduce greenhouse gas emissions through the provision of resilient and low-carbon basic services in the energy sector (installing solar panels on public buildings), promoting sustainable transport, and using green construction materials, collectively resulting in a reduction of 125,032 t CO₂ eq over the lifetime of the investments.

Scale: N/A

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project is a paradigm shift in upgrading climate-vulnerable informal settlements. Supporting planning and regulatory environments will change the paradigm of public sector investment in urban infrastructure development. The project has the potential to lead to increased green investment, adding broader implementation of the project's demonstrated work. The project will also have significant potential for scaling up and replication through project investment, focusing on scaling up and replication, knowledge-sharing, enabling environment creation, and institutional strengthening. The establishment of a TVET Centre will facilitate long-term knowledge-sharing on climate-resilient construction techniques, with an emphasis on replicability, scalability, and sustainability in upscaling and replication. Awareness-raising, citizen engagement, and a knowledge management system will promote knowledge-sharing across Kigali and Rwanda, encouraging citizens to adopt measures that are environmentally sustainable.

The Government of Rwanda will support project replication, with a design template and project manual promoting socially sustainable housing projects.

Scale: N/A

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The project aims to achieve several SDGs through several sustainable development goals (SDG 6, 7, 8, 11 and 11) that result in environmental benefits such as enhancing groundwater recharge, improving water quality, promoting walking and cycling to reduce emissions, and reducing solid waste. The project will also have significant social health outcomes, especially for women and children. Street lighting, rain drainage systems, and clean cooking technologies will further enhance community health and safety. Economic benefits include increased employment opportunities in the construction sector and other sustainable sectors through public infrastructure investments. The project will also provide job training, particularly for women and youth, and promote gender equality by encouraging leadership in construction. Gender-sensitive impacts include reducing domestic burdens on women, creating safe public spaces, and promoting equal access to education and leadership in economic activities. Overall, the project's co-benefits include environmental preservation, social well-being, economic growth, and gender equity.

Scale: N/A

3.4 Needs of the recipient

20 The project effectively addresses the vulnerability and financing needs of Rwanda by targeting its high susceptibility to climate impacts such as increased rainfall variability, prolonged dry spells, landslides, and rising temperatures. The proposed interventions, including constructing water storage facilities, enhancing stormwater management, and promoting low-emission transport solutions, are designed to provide long-term resilience while ensuring that the project generates a positive net present pay while addressing the immediate needs of its vulnerable populations. Rwanda’s limited domestic financial resources and high poverty rates (33%) exacerbate this vulnerability. The country's economic development is heavily dependent on climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture, which are key for climate-compatible development. Thus, international climate finance, such as the GCF grant, is vital to Rwanda to meet its climate resilience and adaptation needs without increasing its debt burden.

The project integrates with the National Strategy for Transformation (NST), the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), and the City of Kigali Master Plan. It integrates with the country’s broader development agenda and builds institutional capacity for sustainable urban development. Leveraging the enabling policy and institutional environment, the project is aligned with Rwanda’s climate change policies such as the National Strategy for Transformation and the National Adaptation Plan. The involvement of key government institutions, including the Ministry of Environment (MINEN), the Ministry of Energy (MINE) FONERWA, ensures strong country ownership and alignment with Rwanda's climate and development priorities. The project also involves extensive stakeholder consultations, ensuring it reflects the needs and priorities of the most vulnerable.

# Environmental and social safeguards

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The review of the Green City Kigali Project has confirmed the AE’s suggested categorization of the project as Category B (moderate risk) under GCF’s environmental and social safeguards policy and system. The project will have multiple positive impacts for improved social conditions and elements of environmental protection (green-right-of-way, nature-based adaptation solutions). The Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) uses a hybrid methodology and comprehensively cover environmental and social risks and impacts. However, the preparation of the ESMF is now 2-3 years old and will be updated in Q1/2021. Due to the timeframe of the appraisal process, these documents are now 2-3 years old. The Secretariat has committed to provide an update and revision of the core ESMF documents immediately after project start and the second disbursement to be conditional upon delivery of such updated documents.

### Project overview

The Project is a solar mini-grid expansion in Nyagirinya Village that extends over 18ha on Kininyi Hill in Gasabo district in Kigali city, Rwanda. The proposed project will also support the installation of a solar PV mini-grid to pilot renewable energy supply at the Nyamugari Health Center. These investments in both sites are part of a much larger development scheme (a 400ha Green City Kigali) project designated by the Government of Rwanda to demonstrate a model of climate responsive urban development and sustainable land use planning for the entire site and replicated across Rwanda and the region. The project will also include activities to build micro-hydro power plants, water harvesting systems, and other renewable energy infrastructure for vulnerable urban communities. The project is expected to generate positive environmental impacts through improved access to clean drinking water, enhanced stormwater management and rainwater harvesting systems, improved access to water and electricity, and the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.

The review of the Green City Kigali Project has confirmed the AE’s suggested categorization of the Project into Category B (moderate risk) under GCF’s environmental and social safeguards policy and system. The ESMF uses a hybrid methodology and comprehensively cover environmental and social risks and impacts. However, the preparation of the ESMF is now 2-3 years old and will be updated in Q1/2021. Due to the timeframe of the appraisal process, these documents are now 2-3 years old. The Secretariat has committed to provide an update and revision of the core ESMF documents immediately after project start and the second disbursement to be conditional upon delivery of such updated documents.

### Environmental and social management framework

The review of the Green City Kigali Project has confirmed that the likelihood environmental and social impacts of the project will be moderate (Category B) given the implementation of mitigation measures. The Secretariat confirms the assigned risk category, which is within the E&S risk accreditation scope of the AE. As the project proposed for the GCF funding is a hybrid methodology, it involves both an Environmental and Social Management Framework and various funding partners, the AE has prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) that will be used to manage the environmental and social risks associated with this project. This involves higher E&S risk activities. In addition to an environmental and social management framework, the Secretariat has also developed a resettlement action plan (RAP) and stakeholder engagement plan (SEP). The ESMF

### ES3 (assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts)

The ES3 identifies that the potential negative impacts and risks are mostly confined to the local environment and include: loss of biodiversity; soil erosion; sedimentation; pollution of soil and water sources; air quality; noise and vibration; impact on surface and groundwater resources; land use change; displacement of communities; social disruption; community health, safety and security risks; potential adverse socio-economic impacts; and habitat and species loss. To identify the environmental and social risks and vulnerabilities analysis, the ES3 envisions land tenure conflicts in the project area and develops conflict resolution strategies as a resettlement action plan to ensure that the project will engage with communities involved and affected by the project. The ES3 also identifies that it is necessary for stakeholders to clearly land ownership and usage rights; implement compensation and resettlement plans; and provide adequate information on the project. These processes are transparent, inclusive, and fair. Secretariat acknowledges that the safeguards documentation is prepared in line with the IFC’s 2016 E&S Framework. The ES3 also identifies that the project goes beyond the upgrading of informal resettlements in the Nanyaruka Village. Funding from GCF is allocated to support the development of a new settlement site for displaced persons, which is a new greenfield commercial and residential project located outside the Nanyaruka Village. Therefore, it is recommended that the Secretariat review the ES3 to ensure that there are no environmental and social due diligence matches with the activities proposed for GCF financing in both sites (Ilha greentiful and Ilha greentiful). It is also noted that ESAF, RAP and SEP were developed based on the project as it was conceived at the time of their development. Since then, some baseline information and contextual circumstances may have changed in the past three years. Therefore, Secretariat should review the ES3 to ensure that there are no environmental and social due diligence matches with the activities proposed for GCF financing in both sites (Ilha greentiful and Ilha greentiful). The AE is recommended to conduct further consultation to confirm whether there is any major change in the project.

### ES2 (labour working conditions)

The ES2 identifies a series of risks faced by project workers, including injuries during construction of infrastructure and settlement upgrading; lack of access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities; inadequate income needed, and discrimination and gender-based violence on sites as the project is targeting women. The ES2 also identifies that the project will provide training for workers in respect and protect the fundamental rights of workers by following the International Labor Organization (ILO) conventions and standards. The ES2 also identifies that the project will provide the project workers understandable documentation of employment terms and conditions, including their rights under national law on hours of work, wages, overtime compensation, leave entitlements, and other benefits. The ES2 also identifies that the project measures to establish and maintain a safe working environment including supply chain workers’ safety training; provision of personal protective equipment (PPE); local emergency procedure (LEMP) and OHS plan at each site to identify physical hazards associated with the work involved such as dust, noise, hazardous substances, and other risks. The ES2 also identifies exposure to dust, noise and hazardous substance that may be present in the demolition waste, construction materials, and other project activities.

### ES3 (resource efficiency and pollution control)

The ES3 identifies that the project may lead to dust and noise during the construction; wastes such as soil, wood chips, metal scrap and other construction debris will be generated. The ES3 also identifies that the project operation; clearance of 34,830 m³ vegetation covered by crops and out of 164 trees; polluted water from the construction site; dust and noise during construction activities; contamination and odour nuisance from base camp connected with sanitation facilities. To minimize these risks, the project will put into place a waste management plan to collect and segregate all construction debris and other wastes generated by the project in order to maintain the soil structure in the project area; maintain low dust levels during construction; install

### ES4 (community health, safety and security)
The project aims to decrease risks of natural disasters and other emergencies in the project area. The ESIA identifies potential negative impacts on communities, including exposure to disease, effects on ecosystem services and hazardous materials, and possible accidents or emergencies on any of the project activities. The ESIA also discusses the need for implementing an emergency management plan and include security requirements in the community health, safety and security (CHSS) section of the EIA. The CHSS will be approved by the AE. The project will also avoid vehicle movements at night, use of silencers for machinery work, etc., to reduce noise pollution. The ESIA also discusses the need to provide access routes to avoid settlements where possible. The ESMP also requires including the security requirements in the CHSS section of the EIA and ensuring that the Voluntary Principles on Human Rights and ESIA requirements are addressed. In case the Project activities include prevention of malaria spread, pesticides might have an impact on the environment.

### ES5 (land acquisition and involuntary resettlement)
The ESIA identifies land acquisition and involuntary resettlement as a significant negative impact. The project will result in displacement of 11 residential houses, 15 commercial structures, 7 residences will be partially affected, and 394 households will be economically displaced. The AE has submitted a separate resettlement action plan (RAP) for the project. The RAP identifies the resources to be mobilised for the expropriation and all accompanying measures for the project site acquisition. It also includes the compensation of displaced persons and structures, resettlement of displaced persons (DPSs) or persons affected by the project (PAPs), and loss of land rights. The RAP will provide a detailed description of how the project will address all individuals having formal legal rights to the land lost and occupants, regardless of ownership of the land. The ESIA also discusses the need for the AE to submit a resettlement action plan in the project's resettlement plan. All DPSs will be given notice in sufficient advance and will be requested to vacate premises and dismantle affected structures prior to project implementation. The RAP will provide details on how compensation of displaced persons and structures, including land and private properties will be carried out in accordance with Rwanda Expropriation Law for purposes of public utility projects and the Rwanda Public Utilities Act of Construction works. The RAP is of high quality and comprehensively covers issues that would be required to address resettlement. The ESIA also discusses the need to identify a substantive issue regarding the land tenure conflicts and monitor land tenure issues throughout the project lifecycle. The ESIA recommends that the AE should establish an independent body for continuously assessing the effectiveness of the set conflict resolution strategies. The AE is also suggested to ensure all categories of affected households and communities shall be consulted, fully compensated, provided with adequate resettlement assistance, and those with no legally recognized claims (for the informal settlements); and adequate restoration and improvement measures will be taken.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESIA (Indigenous Peoples)

The AE has undertaken a review of the project area to identify indigenous peoples and confirmed that there are no historically marginalized groups found in the project area.

#### ESSB (cultural heritage)

While the AE has assessed that there are no physical cultural heritage sites within the project area, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESIA states that stakeholder engagement should be part of the mitigation measures on how to manage and mitigate any potential impacts. The AE will ensure that these procedures to ensure all chance finds of cultural heritage (e.g., graves, old cemeteries, old building fragments) are reported immediately to the relevant authority. Where possible, the project should avoid areas with known or suspected cultural heritage sites.

#### Implementation arrangements

The AE describes the roles and responsibilities among the AI and the executing agencies on the implementation of the project's safeguard instruments. The project will have a Project Management Unit (PMU) consisting of a project manager who will be in charge of the overall Project Coordination. The GCF Environmental and Social Management (ESM) Specialist will be hired to oversee the environmental and social safeguards, including the management of all safeguard components.

#### Stakeholder grievance redressal mechanism (GRR)

The AE has conducted stakeholder consultations during the design phase of the project from 2019 to 2021. These consultations were aimed at identifying key stakeholders and understanding their concerns. The GCR pilot site, the AI is suggested to keep the SGP updated and incorporate findings of all stakeholder consultations into the SGP. For the implementation stage of the project, the appropriate structure for handling grievances includes the AE's GRR which is the umbrella structure that will provide a grievance redressal mechanism. The GRR will provide a survivor-centred process to accommodate serious grievances, and facilitate dialogue between the parties involved in the dispute. The GRR will have a survivor-centered project-level procedure available to all project staff, participants, and beneficiaries who wish to integrate their systems for handling of grievances as appropriate.

#### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH)

# Gender policy

The AIF has provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF gender policy. The project aims to provide a new model for urban development in Rwanda that is inclusive of women and girls.

## Key challenges faced by women, including poverty, limited access to resources and basic services, restricted financial services and employment opportunities, lack of decision-making power and access to education and health care

### Gender-based violence
- **Violence against women**: Women are more likely than men to be victims of gender-based violence.
- **Limited decision-making powers**: Women often have less influence in public spaces and decision-making roles compared to men. This is further exacerbated by gender disparities in decision-making, financial autonomy and mobility restrictions.

### Gender equality
- The project will provide training for women on leadership capacity building, establishing a gender mainstreaming committee, and ensuring women's participation in all aspects of the project.
- It will also engage various stakeholders to promote women’s equal access to resources and services. This includes providing trainings and providing women and girls with essential skills such as riding bicycles, enhancing their mobility.

### Gender action plan
The gender action plan defines activities, indicators and targets for women's participation and benefit with corresponding timelines, budget and gender expertise.

### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (low risk)

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (low risk)
64. The project aims to upgrade the Nganyinka Village informal settlement on Kinyinya Hill with climate-resilient infrastructure and promote renewable energy in the nearby GIK pilot development area. The project will also implement a community-based, participatory, and locally climate-responsive model applicable across Rwanda and the region, emphasizing community resilience and capacity building.

The project has three main outputs: (i) climate-responsive investments to upgrade the informal settlement, providing access to clean water, sanitation, and energy; (ii) an environmental impact assessment (EIA); and (iii) increased capacity for inclusive climate-scenario upgrades and enabling an environment for sustainable development. The total cost of the project is USD 29,029,682, with a grant component of USD 23,602,590 from GCF as a grant, with additional co-financing totaling USD 5,427,092 from the Rwanda Ministry of Finance, resulting in a co-financing ratio of approximately 1:1.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capacity to execute the current programme (low risk)
65. The Ministry of Environment (MOE), serving as the EE for this project, will assume responsibility for overall project management, supervision and reporting to GCF. FONERWA acting as the AE will implement and execute the project. FONERWA was established in 2012 and acts both as an executing entity and a development agency. It is responsible for managing the environmental and climate finance landscape of Rwanda, channeling, programming, disbursing, and monitoring funds in this sector. The AE has two approved projects with GCF under implementation: (i) the Forest Ecosystems and Landscapes Restoration (FELR) project to reduce Climate Variability in Rwanda's Congo Nile Divide through Forest Landscape Restoration; and (ii) FPR2120, which aims to improve access to clean water for 54,387 people. Based on the proven track record, the AE is expected to implement this project in accordance with the requirements of the GCF agreement. The MOE has not yet signed the Letter of Understanding (LoU) for the funded activity agreement, which is still under development. FONERWA has a proven track record and is currently the EE for FPR2120, which is under implementation in the country.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (low risk)
- **Materials sourcing and compatibility challenge:** Based on the project scope as outlined by the GCF proposal, there are concerns about the availability of materials due to the limited availability of local green building materials and potential mismatches in solar PV and water harvesting systems. The AE has identified a number of suppliers who meet the specifications but not fully aligning with final design needs or installation complexities. The AE acknowledges that this is an ongoing challenge, and it will continue to engage with material providers to source suitable materials; (ii) engaging stakeholder input for system adjustments; and (iii) ensuring that all stakeholders are aware of the project's requirements.
- **Project viability [and concessionality]:** The use of grants (100 percent concessionally) is reasonable given the country context. The funding proposal highlights that Rwanda faces significant challenges, including a high population density, limited rainfall, and prolonged dry seasons, which exacerbate rural stabilization challenges. Additionally, the project area experiences frequent landslides due to heavy rainfall intensity making it prone to landslides and floods, impacting housing, health facilities, schools, and infrastructure. The limited availability of affordable housing and local green building materials further complicates the situation. Given these factors, the use of grants is justified, considering Rwanda's dependence on international climate finance due to constrained domestic finances

## Results monitoring and reporting

This is an adaptation project that will contribute to GCF ARAs 1, 2, and 3. In addition, the project has a strong mitigation benefit. The project is expected to reach 39,252 direct beneficiaries, and 1,132,686 indirect beneficiaries. It also anticipates a GHG impact of 125,032 Gg CO₂.

The results management arrangement proposed under the project were refined over four rounds of review and intensive engagement with the AE. Specifically, the project was completed in a manner that is consistent with the project theory of change and the logic framework originally proposed as a cross-cutting project. Changes were made to the project theory of change to ensure that it is fully coherent with the revised project design and the revised activities proposed under the project.

The project logical framework was also refined over multiple rounds to ensure that the proposed activities are both realistic and linked to the proposed theory of change. The AE was also provided advice for refining the supporting annexes, including the Budget, Implementation Plan, Monitoring and Evaluation Plan, feasibility calculations, and mitigation calculations.

A consistent observation throughout the project was the need to ensure consistency across the various documents and plans that were developed as part of the project. This included a theory of change, logical framework, timeline, and monitoring and evaluation plan. Intensive engagement was undertaken with the AE to address this point throughout the review process.

(b) Signature by the accredited entity of an undertaking to provide a copy of such certificate or legal opinion to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date the accredited entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals; and
