# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP245

## Proposal name: Green City Kigali: a new model for urban development in Rwanda

### Accredited entity: Ministry of Environment

### Country: Republic of Rwanda

### Project size: Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview

1. **Rwanda is highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to its geographic location, topography, and reliance on rain-fed agriculture**. The country has seen significant climatic changes in recent years, including increased temperatures and altered rainfall patterns.
2. According to the Rwanda Environment Management Authority, the average temperature has increased by approximately 1.4 °C since the 1970s, which has serious implications for agricultural productivity and food security. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change projects that these trends will continue, leading to more frequent and severe extreme weather events.
3. Agriculture, which forms a substantial part of Rwanda's gross domestic product (GDP) and employs about 70 per cent of the population, is particularly at risk. Reduced agricultural productivity due to climate change could lead to food insecurity and malnutrition among smallholder farmers dependent on rain-fed crops.
4. In urban areas like Kigali, the capital city, drought stress has become more pronounced as temperatures rise and rainfall patterns change. This has led to increased water stress in informal settlements, particularly in informal communities. These challenges are exacerbated by rapid urbanization and lack of infrastructure.
5. Flooding, another major hazard exacerbated by climate change, is particularly severe in Rwanda due to the country's hilly terrain and insufficient drainage infrastructure. Floods destroy property, disrupt transportation networks, and cause significant economic losses.
6. Kigali faces unique challenges related to flooding, with rapid urbanization leading to the expansion of urban areas into flood-prone zones. This has resulted in increased vulnerability to flooding, which can cause significant damage and pose public health risks, such as outbreaks of diseases like cholera, especially in overcrowded settlements.

#### 5. Against this background, the Ministry of Environment (MoE) of the Government of Rwanda requested funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) under the GCF Call for Proposals (2013-27,995,786) to co-finance a project entitled "Green City Kigali: a new model for urban development in Rwanda" (the "GCK Ngaraguru Project").


---
**Page 87**
---

# Impact potential

## Scale: Medium-High

The funding proposal highlights a range of anticipated impacts, most significantly in the adaptation result areas (ARAs) but also encompassing notable mitigation benefits. Specifically, the livelihoods and well-being of vulnerable communities will be improved through:

(a) Strengthened adaptive capacity, heat stress mitigation, and reduced exposure to climate-related hazards such as flooding, landslides, and heat stress in the project area (Ngaruyinka Village). This involves the modernization of public infrastructure and services, including water supply systems, sanitation facilities, and waste management; and

(b) Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and development. Specific project activities are designed to strengthen the ability of government and institutional entities to design, oversee and execute development projects that promote sustainable land use and economic growth, thus facilitating a scale-up of the proposed model project at Ngaruyinka Village.

These two outcomes map to the GCF result areas as follows:


---
**Page 88**
---

### GCF Outcome Metric 1: Total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries among vulnerable people and communities, with the overall target to be direct at project inception (ARA) but currently estimated to be 30,252 direct and 1,132,686 indirect beneficiaries. This includes 47,984 households benefiting from improved water supply, renewable energy, and enhanced capacity to manage climate risks.

### GCF Outcome Metric 2: 1,634 beneficiaries benefiting from improved living conditions through the construction of 50 km of roads, 5.4 km of walking and cycling pathways, and 735 physical structures; and

### GCF Outcome Metric 3: Value of assets that are more resilient to climate change increases by $21 million.

12. In terms of enhancing the enabling environment, all the experts that project will strengthen institutional and technical capacity for planning low-emission, climate-resilient investments in Rwanda. The project is expected to support the development of national building standards (Core Indicator 5). In addition, the AE points that the dissemination of knowledge and skills on climate change adaptation and mitigation will be one of the approaches and solutions developed through the GCF-supported project (Gore Indicator 8).

13. The project is also expected to support low-emission sustainable development by achieving energy savings in buildings and households. This will be achieved with the installation of solar streetlights (saving 76.2 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent per annum (CO2-eq/annum) compared to conventional lighting systems), as well as LED lights for community households (saving 0.9 CO2-eq/ha per household). Emissions are also expected to be cut by 25% in the construction sector, with a reduction of 143 tonnes of CO2-eq/annum as a result of the use of green construction materials. Additionally, the project's Technical Vocational Education and Training (TVET) initiative will lower building energy intensity and operational emissions through the promotion of an efficient design with energy use under 20 kilowatt hour/month.

14. The AE and EE expect to accomplish these outcomes via eight outputs across the two main outcome areas, namely:

### Outcome 1: Strengthened adaptive capacity; heat stress mitigation and reduced exposure to climate risks

(a) Output 1: Strengthened adaptive capacity; heat stress mitigation and reduced exposure to climate risks;

(b) Output 2: Implement resilient green transport/mobility networks;

(c) Output 3: Implement resilient storm water management systems using nature-based solutions;

(d) Output 4: Expand, install and ensure resilience of central water supply and rainwater harvesting systems;

(e) Output 5: Promotion and implementation of renewable energy and energy-efficient technologies.

### Outcome 2: Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and development

(f) Output 6: Institutional capacity development for Government of Rwanda agencies/institutions;


---
**Page 89**
---

### Output 7: Blueprinting the Green City Kigali and developing planning codes for the Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV)

#### Output 8: Awareness-raising and citizen engagement for climate action

The logical framework presented in the funding proposal is comprehensive and strategically aligned with the project’s objectives. However, it should be noted that there are various factors that may negatively impact the ability of the proposed project to generate the projected benefits.

### Potential for land and property speculation
The AE proposes to fully finance the Nyagirinya Village upgrade project with grants for projects to prevent the cost of infrastructure investments from increasing in the future. This could lead to a significant increase in living conditions and better access to public services. This could result in a significant shift in ownership over time, as some community members may choose to sell their land or homes to take advantage of increased property values.

### Calculation of indirect beneficiaries
The calculation of indirect beneficiaries in the funding proposal is based on the assumption that all residents of Nyagirinya Village will benefit from the project. In the view of the IFAP, extending the pool of indirect beneficiaries to residents of Kigali City who may also benefit from the infrastructure improvements and various service offerings provided by the project in Nyagirinya Village seems justifiable, whereas including all residents of Kigali entirely rests on the notion that (i) the technology will be available for use across the city; (ii) there are no existing or planned informal settlements across Kigali; and (iii) that Kigali residents will be able to enroll in the TSET school system. The IFAP recommends that the calculation of indirect beneficiaries should be based on conservative estimates to avoid over-claiming the number of beneficiaries.

### Operation and maintenance (O&M) plan
The AE, in the funding proposal as well as during the Q4 process with the ITAP acknowledges the need for a long-term financing strategy for O&M. However, there is no detailed strategy or plan in place regarding the proposed project. The proposal, however, lacks a detailed strategy of how long-term maintenance will be funded and managed. It also does not provide any guidance on leading over responsibility to local stakeholders while ensuring monitoring of compliance will work in practice. This should be a key priority during the early project execution phase. The City of Kigali will be responsible for shouldering the bulk of the maintenance costs in future, without being able to generate sufficient revenue from the project to cover these costs. Therefore, it is financially contributable (e.g., increased taxes or user fee). For this proposed pilot project, there are additional responsibilities that need to be considered. The long-term maintenance required by the AE will have to consider a sustainable strategy for financing O&M of new public infrastructure.

### Sustainability of solar PV systems
As part of the proposed project design, 224 households in Nyagirinya Village will receive solar PV systems, solar water heaters and improved cookstoves.


---
**Page 90**
---

# Free of charge, these installations are then supposed to drive some of the key mitigation co-benefits highlighted above. While the ITAP recognizes the limited ability of the target households to pay, experience with programmes in developing countries where such systems were provided free of charge has shown that this is not always a sustainable approach.

## Sustainability of systems: Free provision has frequently resulted in lack of ownership, where communities may not invest their own resources in replacing the technology at the end of its useful life. This can lead to premature replacement due to degradation and failure of systems over time. Access to spare parts and technical support is also often limited or non-existent.

### Inadequate training and awareness: In some cases, insufficient training and awareness-raising among recipients have resulted in the improper use of a system or a failure to maintain it properly. This can lead to premature replacement due to degradation and failure of systems over time. Access to spare parts and technical support is also often limited or non-existent.

#### Funding and scalability: Programmes typically require substantial upfront investment, often from donor agencies, before they become financially sustainable. However, scaling up such initiatives to reach larger populations can be challenging, especially when concessional funding is not available.

##### Social marketing: Supply-side subsidies, such as providing goods or services for free, can lead to the distribution of items that may not be wanted or needed by the recipients. In contrast, demand-side subsidies, which provide financial incentives directly to consumers through campaigns highlighting the benefits of the product or service, are more likely to be adopted and used effectively.

###### Promoting the advantages of fuel-efficient cookstoves - both for household savings and forest conservation - is a key challenge in scaling up these technologies. Through demand-side subsidies, households can receive financial support to buy a stove that meets their needs, potentially from a local producer, thereby fostering a market relationship.

In conjunction, the challenges identified above may mean that the expected mitigation co-benefits of such programmes are not fully realized. In addition to the sustainability issues in the initial pilot area, additional issues around dependency and market distortion would have to be considered if these technologies were scaled up beyond the immediate project area, which could undermine the development of a local market for these technologies. It can also distort the market by discouraging private sector investment and local entrepreneurship, as people may wait for government subsidies rather than investing in their own projects.

At the same time, there are opportunities to address some of these challenges through the ITAP's proposed project. For example, the ITAP could provide resources for establishing a mini-grid based on solar PV at the pilot site. This would not only demonstrate the potential benefits beyond the immediate project area, it will be important to properly scope such a project given the inherent complexity and scale involved.

Overall, the ITAP rates the impact potential for this project as medium high with potential for improvement if the issues highlighted above are adequately clarified and addressed prior to the start of programme implementation.

## Paradigm shift potential: Scale: Medium


---
**Page 91**
---

25. Overall, A4R outlines a plausible approach for the project to catalyse a paradigm shift by fostering conditions for scaling and replication, essentially via the following "transmission belts":
   - Establishing a Technical Vocational Education Training Centre that is focused on climate-resilient construction will enhance long-term knowledge-sharing and building, driving the scaling and replication of project activities;
   - National-level institutional arrangements, including a national coordination mechanism alongside a knowledge management system, will encourage broader adoption of project measures, such as rainwater harvesting, across Kigali and Rwanda.
   - District-level institutional arrangements will provide local support, providing future developers with sustainable design tools to scale up affordable, climate-resilient housing projects;
   - Enhancing the planning and regulatory framework will create an enabling environment, attracting investment for the green upgrade of informal settlements, and supporting broader replication.

26. Knowledge-sharing, awareness-raising, design templates, and an enhanced enabling environment will be important contributors to replication of the project results but may not be sufficient on their own. The following sections provide a more detailed discussion that highlights some aspects that may further improve the project's potential to effect paradigm shift:

   - Exploration of public-private collaboration models: Scaling the project on Kinyinya Road (and other sites in the city) will require substantial financial investment. While the factors mentioned above may enhance the enabling environment for private sector participation (e.g., through improved infrastructure), the funding proposal lacks a clear strategy for mobilizing future investment for replication. The project team should consider exploring public-private collaboration models where the private sector is involved, as public sector resources alone will not suffice. Although the AE indicated during the Q&A process that traditional public-private collaboration models would be explored during the project implementation phase, this does not appear to have been done yet. The A4R recommends systematically exploring future financing models, particularly publicly-private collaborative approaches.

   - Urban design and regulation: For the purposes of replication, the ITAP would also like to emphasize the importance of Activity 2.1.3: Strengthening planning and regulatory frameworks. This activity is critical for ensuring that the enabling environment (e.g., land use regulations) will be in place when designs stand as the best direct way for establishing pathways "to ensure replication and scale up" (A4R, p. 50).

   - Area-based monitoring: The project emphasizes a holistic approach aiming to comprehensively changes across various levels and sectors rather than focusing on individual interventions. While the monitoring framework described in Section 3 of this annex is presented in annex 4, focuses on monitoring of individual activities, which may not effectively capture the overall impact of the project. A more comprehensive monitoring framework (e.g., a hybrid approach using satellite data combined with ground sensors) during project planning as clarified during the Q&A session would be more appropriate for capturing the holistic nature of this concept and enable real-time monitoring of total impacts of the project. The AE chose a more traditional activity-based monitoring approach for this pilot phase, most likely due to budget constraints. However, it is recommended that the project team consider exploring an area-based monitoring approach could provide more compelling data and evidence to demonstrate the project's overall impact


---
**Page 92**
---

36. Overall, the ITAP rates the potential for the project to effect paradigm shift as medium.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High

The funding proposal highlights a broad range of sustainable development benefits:

- Environmental co-benefits (including but not limited to):
    - Stormwater management: Enhancement of groundwater recharge and wetland water quality
    - Rainwater harvesting: Reduction of pressure on surface water sources; and Green mobility: Promotion of walking and cycling, improving air quality.
- Social co-benefits (including but not limited to):
    - Water access: Reduction of time and money women and children spend on collecting water
    - Clean cooking: Improving health by reducing reliance on firewood; and Street lighting: Enhancing safety, especially for women.
- Economic co-benefits (including but not limited to):
    - Urbanization: Improved urban planning and increased productivity;
    - Infrastructure investment: Raising land values and attracting private investment; and Job creation: VET and composting initiatives create employment opportunities.
- Gender-sensitive development impact (including but not limited to):
    - Safe spaces: Reduction of gender-based violence through women-friendly public spaces; and
    - Empowerment: Increasing women’s participation in community leadership.

The funding proposal does not provide a detailed exposition of how to practice these co-benefits. The ITAP rates the potential for the project to effect paradigm shift as medium, with insufficient detail to determine whether these potential co-benefits will be tracked during project implementation and reporting. It is noted that the framework for monitoring and evaluation has been clarified that the project will leverage FONERWA’s established M&E framework; it is unclear however how this framework will be adapted to the specific context of the project, or even whether that Framework will have to be amended. The ITAP recommends that this be clarified early on in project execution.

Overall, the ITAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of the project as high.

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

Rwanda faces a high intensity of exposure to climate risks due to its mountainous terrain, reliance on rain-fed agriculture, and increasingly significant variations in rainfall patterns. The country is experiencing more frequent and severe droughts, such as floods, landslides and droughts, which are becoming more frequent and severe due to climate change. Additionally, Rwanda is exposed to slow onset events like prolonged droughts and temperature increases that can have a long-term impact on the environment and overall food security. The country’s degree of vulnerability is exacerbated by its economic dependence


---
**Page 93**
---

# Rwanda's National Determined Contributions (NDCs) Update 2020: Government of Rwanda

Rwanda is classified by the World Bank as a low-income country with a gross national income per capita of approximately USD $850 in 2019. Over the past two decades, Rwanda has achieved remarkable progress, including a GDP growth rate of 6% per year on average. Recent years, however, have been challenging due to the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic.

Rwanda's climate finance needs are outlined in its nationally determined contributions (NDCs), where it estimates the total cost of implementing its climate action plans (both mitigation and adaptation). According to the NDCs, Rwanda will need approximately USD $3.5 billion by 2030 for fully implementing its climate action plan. The government has already allocated USD $18 million during the ten-year period.

The overall financing gap for the project is estimated at USD $6.5 billion for the 2020-30 period. This amounts to roughly 40 percent of Rwanda's Annual GDP (based on 2023 figures).

The Government of Rwanda has made a pledge to finance 40 percent of the expected climate change mitigation and adaptation costs through domestic resources by 2030, while implementing various initiatives to enhance domestic resource mobilization and drive efficiency in public budgeting and execution. However, following the COVID-19 pandemic, the government's ability to meet its pledge has been significantly impacted.

Pursuing Rwanda's ambitious climate agenda may pose more risks to debt sustainability and could potentially lead to a significant increase in the country’s debt-to-GDP ratio.

Rwanda's development partners, including CCF and the multilateral development banks, are playing an important role in supporting the country's climate adaptation and mitigation efforts. They have provided technical assistance and policy advice to help Rwanda implement its climate agenda. These efforts include capacity-building initiatives, strengthening institutional frameworks, enhancing transparency, and capacity-building initiatives.

Additionally, partners have supported the establishment and strengthening of Rwanda’s Green Climate Fund (Rwanda Green Fund), to ensure that climate investments are effectively managed and can be scaled up in a sustainable manner. The Rwanda Green Fund is one of the broader GCF projects which encompass the development of a 600 hectare area at Kinyinya Hill.

Against that backdrop, while relatively modest in overall terms, the proposed GCF funding for the Rwanda Green Project can make an important contribution to promoting Rwanda's ambitious climate agenda. Overall, the project has the potential to serve the needs of the recipients as high.

1.6 Country ownership

### Scale: Medium-High

#### Coherence with existing policies:
The proposed project is aligned with national climate change priorities of the Government of Rwanda, entitled to NDC and to the National Strategy for Climate Resilience Strategy. The required no-objection letter has been submitted by the NDA.

#### Capacity of the EE:
FONERWA, the Rwanda Green Fund, has established itself as a key player in the country's climate finance landscape, with several accomplishments in driving climate action. FONERWA's experience and capacity have been instrumental in attracting significant international climate finance, securing funds from key development partners such as the UK’s International Climate Fund and the World Bank (TFIP) “Strategic Climate Fund” (SCF) and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). This has enabled the financing of a wide range of climate-related projects across Rwanda. The TFIP is an important source of funding for FONERWA, as it provides grants to support the implementation of projects in developing countries.

In the context of the proposed project there are two areas in which FONERWA’s capacity could be reinforced and/or enhanced:

(a) Monitoring and evaluation:
The suitability of FONERWA's monitoring and evaluation processes should be reassessed and potentially enhanced. While FONERWA's Monitoring, Evaluation, Learning and Improvement (MELI) framework is currently prioritizing capturing immediate project outputs rather than deeply assessing long-term outcomes and impacts, the use of more advanced monitoring techniques such as remote sensing and participatory monitoring technologies, it may be beneficial to update and improve FONERWA’s monitoring processes. This would enable a better understanding of the impact on the private sector.

(b) Private sector engagement:
Although FONERWA has made strides in engaging the private sector (including the renewable energy, agriculture and tourism sectors), the level of engagement is still insufficient for ensuring that the project contributes to the overall climate finance needs of the country. There is potential to further explore innovative ways of engaging with the private sector, including through co-investment in climate action, including in the context of the proposed project, and FONERWA could potentially benefit from additional capacity development in that area, especially with a view to supporting the implementation of the private sector component of the proposed project.

### Environmental and social action plan (ESAP) and resettlement action plan (RAP):
An initial environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and RAP were concluded as part of project planning in 2021 and submitted as annexes to the funding proposal (annexes 6a and 6b). In addition, a high-level Environmental and Social Impact Assessment Report (annex 6c), a High-Level Environmental and Social Impact Assessment Framework (annex 6d), an Environmental and Social Management Framework (annex 6e) and an Environmental and Social Management Framework (annex fig), with annexes from 6g to 6j covering resettlement, were also submitted as part of the funding proposal.

The ESIA and RAP are now three years old. As a part of the project, the ESIA will have to be updated, and an environmental and social action plan (ESAP) will have to be produced. In addition, the RAP will need to be revised in order to reflect the new project design. Both the ESAP and RAP will have to be implemented. The project budget (annex 4) does not include a dedicated budget for these activities. However, the preparation of an updated ESIA and RAP implementation are both critical to the project's success and explains that funding for these activities will be mobilized from the Project Management Component of the budget.


---
**Page 95**
---

### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium

The economic analysis for this project focuses on evaluating its broader impacts at the household level, including household benefits, carbon savings, and the value of forest preservation (through reduced charcoal use). Payback periods for the various components differ; rainwater harvesting systems take about two years to pay back, while water heaters last up to 30 years for solar PV installations. The CO2 reduction cost for GEF is around USD 224 per tonne of carbon dioxide avoided over the project’s lifetime, amounting to approximately $125,632/CO2 eq reduced over the lifetime of the project, which is very high. However, the core focus of the project is on the anticipated adaptation benefits such as improved infrastructure and water supply, with substantial positive environmental impacts, which are not quantified in the economic analysis.

The feasibility study (attached as annex 2) does not contain a detailed cost-benefit analysis and associated financial projections. The bank has derived the basis for the project cost estimates and it also now three years old. The project budget (annex 4) does contain cost estimates, but these also are based on the same assumptions as those in the feasibility study. These assumptions have significantly shifted upwards (due to supply chain disruptions, increased demand and inflationary pressures). It is important that all stakeholders involved with the project team members are aware of this issue and that the project team is closely monitoring the situation, arguing that any potential cost increases should be reflected in future budget revisions. Based on experience in other projects, the ITAP deems it likely that overall project costs may increase by 15 to 25 per cent in recent years. Thus, the ITAP recommends that the AE consider these factors when developing a revised project implementation to consider the overall impact this may have on the project budget and potential for reallocation of project resources.

The ITAP therefore considers the efficiency and effectiveness of the programme as medium.


### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Overall, the funding proposal is comprehensive, and although there are various risks and weaknesses (as highlighted above) it shows a credible pathway to achieving notable impacts.

The ITAP recommends that the AE:


---
**Page 96**
---

(a) Assesses the potential for land and property speculation in the early phase of project implementation to determine the potential scale of the problem and, if necessary, establish mitigant mechanisms such as community land trusts or long-term lease agreements.

(b) Reviews the calculation methodology for indirect beneficiaries prior to project commencement and ensures a conservative methodology is applied; develops a detailed plan for the distribution of solar panels and solar PV and solar water heater household systems distributed for free, with a clear delineation of roles and responsibilities, as a key priority during the early project execution phase;

(c) draws on lessons learned from other projects in terms of urban standards and urban designs as a key methodology for replication and scale-up of the initiative.

(d) Systematically explores future financing models for scaling up the project results, particularly in relation to the potential for private sector involvement and

(e) Conducts a detailed costing exercise in the early stages of project implementation to consider the overall impact that increased construction costs may have on the project budget and potential need for recalibration of project activities.

The TDFP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

1. **Introduction & Context**

Gender assessments and gender action plans are an integral part of proposals submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The purpose of a gender assessment is to ensure adequate and appropriate attention is given to gender issues and dynamics across and within projects. A gender assessment is meant to ensure the project proposal design is informed by a thorough understanding of gender roles, power relations, and dynamics in the context of vulnerability, resilience, and adaptation. This includes the disaggregation and nuanced understanding of women’s and men’s specific roles, responsibilities, knowledge, needs, interests, and priorities in order for women’s rights and gender equality to be increasingly enhanced throughout the project lifecycle.

A gender assessment uses an interdisciplinary method of gender analysis to understand relationships between women and men over access to resources, their daily activities, and the constraints they face relative to each other. A comprehensive gender analysis involves the examination of the different roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women and men, boys and girls within the specific project or program context. The process of a gender analysis helps to identify and understand the diverse knowledge and capacities within communities that are critical for effective adaptation and resilience strategies in the face of climate shocks and risks, and works to clearly identify gender gaps and opportunities where the effective and influential participation of women and girls must be proactively championed and secured in order for climate-responsive projects to support and benefit them in equal measure to men and boys.

The Gender Policy of the GCF (updated and reviewed in November 2019) explicitly recognizes that gender equality is a key principle of the Fund, and that women and men have equal rights and access to and control over decisions, assets and resources, information, and knowledge. The policy also recognizes that the impacts of climate change can exacerbate existing gender inequalities, and acknowledges that women and men are not equally affected by climate change, especially in terms of their ability to achieve their objectives when gender equality and women’s empowerment considerations are integrated into the design and implementation of projects. The updated GCF Gender Policy also recognizes that women and vulnerable groups have a right to participate in climate change and should, therefore, be effectively engaged in all discussions and decisions that affect them.

In line with the principles and rationale of the GCF Gender Policy, this gender assessment has been conducted as part of the proposal submitted to GCF for the Nguranyika Village Upgrade project in Kigali, Rwanda. This report maps the gender dynamics within the target focus area of Nguranyika Village, draws out between-project differences and similarities with other projects in the same community, gathers men’s and women’s feedback on the proposed project intervention areas, and further identifies the specific gender gaps that are affecting the active participation of women in decision-making processes, as well as future project outcomes, related to the proposed village upgrade. The findings of this report will be used by the project team to inform the design of the project document, which seeks to directly address the gender barriers and gaps outlined in this assessment.

1 Updated Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan 2020-2035, Green Climate Fund, November 6, 2019.

# The Link Between Gender and Climate Change

There is a direct and well-documented link between gender equality, women’s empowerment and climate change. Research shows that women are disproportionately more vulnerable to the effects of climate change, which can result in existing gender disparities becoming even more pronounced and harmful over time. Rapidly advancing climate change will amplify chronic environmental threats, such as water scarcity and food insecurity, which already place the poor at a disadvantage. Due to gender bias and inequalities related to education, economic empowerment, violence and other forms of oppression and vulnerability, women are more likely to be poor than men. Among other disadvantaged groups, such as people living with HIV/AIDS, women are often the poorest of the poor, particularly if they face other challenges such as being a female head of household.

Poor women are especially vulnerable to climate change as their incomes are often highly dependent on natural resources that are sensitive to climate shocks and variability, and in general, women possess fewer assets. Women are predominantly involved in subsistence agriculture, through which they are negatively affected by climate change. They have less access to land, water, seeds, fertilizers, etc., have less economic independence, and are not involved in political or household decision making processes to the same degree that men are. Where they are involved, as baseline data gathered for this report will reveal, women’s work is engaged in a tokonoma manner and their views and experiences are not considered as valuable or significant.

The global gender gap is vast and distinct. Nearly 70% of those who live on less than $1 a day are women. Women also account for 75% of the world's 876 million adults who are illiterate. While women work two-thirds of the world’s total working hours, they receive only 10% of the world’s income, and although women own or manage about one-third of all farms worldwide, they control less than 20%, while owning just 10% of the world's land. In Africa, the proportion of women affected by climate-related crop changes could range from 48% in Burkina Faso to 73% in the Congo. Every year, there are 2 million deaths, mainly women and children, related to the burning of biomass fuels indoors.

A robust body of evidence clearly demonstrates that women and men do not experience climate change equally. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change in many ways in which they are uniquely affected by climate change as a direct result of gender roles and inequalities.

Climate-related disasters

- Research shows that women and children are up to 14 times more likely to die in natural disasters than men, due to their weaker asset base and the lack of resilience they experience as a result.
- Women's vulnerability to climate change and natural disasters is directly related to their economic and social rights; data on climate disasters in 141 countries shows that in countries with greater gender equality, this differential in vulnerability disappears.

Poverty and vulnerability

- Social vulnerability in the context of climate change can be defined as "the inability of individuals or groups to anticipate, cope with and recover from the impact of a natural hazard".
- Poverty and vulnerability are directly linked to climate change; gendered patterns of poverty as well as women's unique social vulnerability put them at greater risk.

While women and girls undoubtedly face unique vulnerabilities related to climate change, and these vulnerabilities are critical to consider and respond to, it is equally important to recognize their agency in adapting to and mitigating climate change. In practical terms, this means recognizing women and girls as equally valuable participants in decision-making processes, as well as designing and implementing real world solutions that address the needs of women or girls. There is already significant evidence of women playing a vital role in effectively mobilizing communities for disaster preparedness, responding to climate disasters and driving social movements to end harmful practices that significantly contribute to climate change.

2.1 Gender and Climate Change in Rwanda

Climate variability due to global warming is already posing specific challenges for Rwanda, including more frequent and intense extreme weather events such as floods and droughts, which have a significant negative impact on natural resources, the economy, food security, and differentiated impacts on women and men. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change and natural disasters in general; women are more impacted by climate change and natural disaster impacts on land tenure and in settlements." An individual's access to assets and resources largely determines their climate vulnerability and capacity to adapt. Women have less access to productive resources, including land, credit, agricultural inputs, access to markets, decision-making bodies, technology and training. This lack of access to productive resources makes women in Rwanda more vulnerable and less resilient to the impacts of climate change.

Women's ability to cope with climate change remains limited due to higher poverty rates for women than for men, lower literacy rates among women, limited access to extension services and different

## The Gender Context of Rwanda

### 3.1 Overview

Over the last one decade, the Government of Rwanda has made considerable efforts to mainstream gender equality and women's empowerment within and across national priorities of the country, including Vision 2050/2020, the Economic Development and Poverty Reduction Strategy (EDPRS) that later became the National Strategy for Transformation (NST), and through the revised National Gender Policy (2021), among others. These strategies have guided and been translated into implementable actions to ensure equal participation of men and women in both public and private sectors of the country's development. The 2003 Constitution of the Republic of Rwanda (amended in 2015) emphasizes gender equality by conferring at least 30% of positions to women in decision-making organs, and other gender sensitive laws have been enacted including mandatory representation for women in public service and land access as well as the Law on the Prevention and Punishment of Gender-based Violence (GBV).

To ensure Gender Responsive Budgeting (GRB) in public institutions. Gender Budget Statements (GBS) have been made for requirement of all budgeting institutions. This has been institutionalized through the promulgation of Organizational Regulations (OR) No. 02/2019 and OR No. 03/2019, which were adopted in 2019.

The law is implemented and overseen by the Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning, through planning and budgeting process. This has enabled actors specifically public institutions to use public resources to benefit both men and women girls and boys and give institutional commitments for gender equality and empowerment. These are led by Gender Monitoring Officers (GMOs) under the Family Promotion Unit (FPU), Gender Monitoring Office (GMO), National Women's Council (NWC) and Rwanda Forum for Women Parliamentarians (FRP), and play a major role as gender mainstreaming focal points in ensuring that policy implementation is gender sensitive, making meaningful contributions to the fight against GBV as well as the empowerment of women and girls for their active participation in the national development process.

Despite these important legal reforms, patriarchal gender norms remain pervasive within Rwandan society, as this assessment will further illustrate. Rwanda, both historically and presently, is a deeply


---
**Page 109**
---

# Patriarchal Culture and Society

Despite the country being lauded for its progress in recent years in terms of legislation, women in government, and national narratives promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls, patriarchal beliefs continue to dominate Rwandan society, particularly at the grassroots level.10 Households and communities in Rwanda in the Rwandan context perpetuate the idea that men are superior to women in most facets of life, and support and reinforce the subjugation of women in various ways as well as men’s dominance over them.11 Women are generally expected to be responsible for household chores such as cooking, cleaning, and childcare, while men are expected to be responsible for virtually all of the domestic and childcare duties, or what is otherwise known more broadly as “unpaid care work.” Men are considered heads of households as well as primary decision makers in family matters. Women often have little say over their children’s education and engage in paid work outside of the home, or other opportunities for personal growth and development. When women do make money, most of the decisions around how, when and why it is spent are made by the husband. Girls in Rwanda are frequently removed from a young age to fulfill traditional roles as adults, perpetuating and further solidifying the validity of these gender norms. This in fact leads to many harmful inequalities over time.

Gender inequality has an impact on all aspects of society, from economic growth to health and well-being of a country’s population more broadly, as well as to climate change mitigation and adaptation.12 Despite being a country of progress regarding gender equality for development, discrimination against women and girls is pervasive around the world, and Rwanda is no exception. While Rwanda’s prominent international image as a champion of gender equality, especially in terms of women in parliament and leadership roles, men continue to dominate all sectors of society, including education, health, business, politics, and other sectors such as health, education, the judiciary, local government, and national government.13 Further, violence against women and girls is another serious concern that has severe negative consequences for their agency, well-being, and development. According to the 2019 Demographic Health Survey (DHS), nationally representative study conducted periodically by the National Institute of Statistics in Rwanda (NISR), reveals that 45% of girls aged 15–19 and 42% of young women aged 20-24 having experienced violence at least once since the age of 15.14 In fact, gendered normative attitudes that support and perpetuate violence against women and girls are also pervasive in Rwanda. According to the most recent Demographic Health Survey (DHS), a nationally representative study conducted periodically by the National Institute of Statistics in Rwanda (NISR), reveals that 45% of girls aged 15–19 and 42% of young women aged 20-24 believe that a husband is justified in beating his wife if she burns food, refuses to have sex with him when he wants it, does not do household chores as expected by her husband, or refuses to have sex with him.15 (Detailed findings from the most recent DHS 2019/20 survey are still pending.)

### Key Metrics

The table below outlines key metrics in Rwanda related to the current gender gap in different areas of social and economic life, with data drawn from recent gender assessments conducted for GCF

# Table 2: Key metrics reflecting the current gender gap in Rwanda

Despite supportive legislation, women continue to face considerable disadvantages in terms of poverty, particularly the very poor and women living in rural areas.

Women are more likely than men to be in decent paid employment working mainly as dependent family labourers, and working significantly longer hours than men on a daily basis, particularly when unpaid care work is taken into account. Women also earn less money for their work compared with men and face even greater challenges. According to ECVS, 39.5% of female headed households are poor compared to 37.6% of male headed households in Rwanda.

Findings from this assessment as well as other similar studies specific to Rwanda indicate that women have limited agency and decision making power when it comes to their agency and decision making power women are able to access within their homes and communities. Women have limited agency in terms of making decisions independently, especially on a household level. The areas where they have the most decision-making power is typically relegated to areas where men consider women to have the most relevant interests, such as childcare or domestic work. Men are more likely than women to be able to make decisions and take action without the involvement of their wives at all, even in such cases as buying parcels of land or livestock without the wife's consent.

In terms of representation, Rwanda has made significant progress with a high number of women in parliament, women’s representation and leadership at the local level and other levels of government is significantly lower and relegated to lower-level positions. Women have also made some positive progress in private sector decision-making, in terms of women’s participation in economic decision making bodies. In 2018, women represented 25% of board members, 34% of CEOs, 27% of senior management staff, and 36-38% of women employees in the private sector.

In 2018, women represented 25% of board members, 34% of CEOs, 27% of senior management staff, and 36-38% of women employees in the private sector. |


---
**Page 111**
---

The 2014/15 DHS indicates that only 23% of women reported being empowered to make decisions independently regarding their own health care, while 16% reported that decisions were mainly made by their husbands. Sexual and gender-based violence is a major barrier to women's empowerment in making decisions about sexual and reproductive health. Cultural norms related to sex within the context of marriage, as well as religious influences, men tend to control decisions around sexual and reproductive health within marriage and both men and women feel that women cannot easily refuse to have sex if the husband demands it.[2]

In the DHS 2014/15 survey, 44% of women reported ever having experienced physical or sexual violence, and 36% reported having experienced injuries due to intimate partner violence in the past twelve months. Despite strong policies and legislation against gender-based violence, many women continue to experience violence. Cultural norms that stress male authority and female submission significantly affect women's likelihood of reporting when they experience male partner violence.

Women’s access to finance continues to lag behind men’s and is hindered by power dynamics in the home and society that affect a woman’s ability to access finance without relying on permission from husbands or having collateral to offer for a loan. Women continue to have lower levels of financial inclusion than men, with financial inclusion from 39% in 2013 to 86% in 2016. Currently, women with savings accounts are estimated at 28.7% and the number of women who borrowed from the banking sector has increased from 54% in 2013 to 70% in 2016. Women continue to access formal financial services at higher rates than women, while women only informal financial services (savings groups, etc.) at higher rates than men. Women’s lack of education and limited literacy levels as well as limited time also affects their ability to access credit programs and services.[3]

Women in Rwanda do vastly more unpaid care work than their male counterparts with 77.2% of women versus 22.8% of men contributing to unpaid care work in the home.[4] Women remain the primary providers of all care work in the home, while men contribute less time and effort, and are responsible for only a small share of all aspects of childcare, as well as domestic work including cleaning, cooking, fetching water and firewood, washing clothes, and taking family members and children to health facilities. Women also spend more time on agricultural labour for the family plot and caring for livestock. Men have significantly more rest and leisure time than women do, and except in rare cases, generally do not contribute to the domestic work load; even when they are unemployed or are only able to access temporary, part-time work.[5]


---
**Page 112**
---

Education

School attendance at the primary level is relatively high for both boys and girls in Rwanda in recent years, while men and women in the older generation have far fewer years of schooling than young people do today, with women having lower levels of education than men and boys. The government has implemented several policies in the education sector related to school attendance for girls; these have helped to close the gender gaps in education, but barriers persist. Trades embraced by girls are often seen as less prestigious or more difficult compared to those embraced by boys, which reinforces gender stereotypes; for example, males dominate within transportation services (93.6% for males versus 5.4% for females), in manufacturing and metal services (females hold 10.8% of jobs compared to 72.8% held by males) and in construction trades (females hold 16.4% compared to 83.6% of males), and in energy (females hold 20.6% compared to 79.4% held by males).11

The drop out rate diverges significantly after age 16, with girls dropping out of school at higher rates than boys, particularly in rural areas, and their re-entry rates are even lower. Girls face numerous challenges related to puberty, missing multiple days of school each month due to lack of access to menstrual hygiene products.

Employment

Women in Rwanda are generally concentrated in unpaid family work, due to gendered expectations related to their roles as wives and mothers. Even if employment is available, women’s access to paid jobs is not equal to that of men's. Men dominate the formal sector (75% of employed males versus 23% for women), while women are more concentrated in low-paying jobs such as farm crop laborers, domestic workers, market salespeople and small-scale traders; among employed individuals with managerial positions, only 32% are women. Unemployment rates are higher in rural areas than urban areas, but women are unemployed at a higher rate (25%) compared to men (19%). The overall unemployment rate increased by 9.3%; the impact was highest among women (25%), followed by youth (18%), and then young adults (16%); for girls aged 16–24 years old, this figure is even higher at 32%. Unemployment rates are also higher among older women (32%) compared to younger adult (17.6%) or 31 years old and higher.12

Women's livelihoods were significantly negatively affected by the COVID-19 pandemic. The majority of women in Rwanda are employed in informal sectors such as small-scale traders, tour guides (domestic and foreign), and domestic workers; retail trade and casual street trade, all of which have been severely impacted by the restriction of movement due to the spread of COVID-19 and the closure of borders. This has resulted in loss of business and a significant number of job losses for women while others were compelled to divert capital toward basic needs of the household.


12 Education Statistical Handbook, MINENDE, 2018.

14 Labour Force Survey, NISR, May 2020.


---
**Page 113**

Land

Recent data shows that reforms in the Land Law and Inheritance Law have enabled more women to own land and, to some degree, use land for collateral. Currently, 59.9% of registered land is owned by married couples, 24% is owned by women as individuals, and 16% is owned by men individually. Women are increasingly buying and credit using their land as collateral and 25.5% women accessed agriculture loans using their land as collateral.[20] However, while women are legally entitled to own land in Rwanda, they face significant challenges in accessing and exercising control over land.

Agriculture

While the agriculture sector provides many of the employment opportunities for men and women in Rwanda, there is a significant gender gap; women make up just 15% of agricultural workers. Women also earn less than men on average, hold informal jobs and far fewer professional or managerial positions than men.[20] 75% of women are self-employed in agriculture, compared with just 63% of men.[20] Due to the heavy workload associated with childcare and household chores, women care for work, women also struggle to access training, resources and opportunities related to agricultural development, and have limited access to and control over financial resources. Women’s lack of access to land and credit is a major barrier that also has a distinct gender gap in women's access to agricultural loans, with women representing just 25.5% and men representing 74.5% of those accessing loan for agriculture.[20]


---
**Page 114**
---

About 40% of Rwanda’s population lives below the poverty line, with women, disabled, widowed, and rural populations disproportionately affected. Adult women are more likely to be living in poverty (34.8%) than adult males (31.6%), and they have less access to land for agriculture inputs due to their low economic capacity. Women’s often challenging situation in terms of land tenure security and access to options for dimensioning their livelihoods is very serious, as women are disproportionately affected by climate change. Unequal gendered power dynamics between men and women cause women to bear most negative effects of climate change. The increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather with an increase in floods and droughts will impact food security and water availability in Rwanda. The fact that women are primarily responsible for households’ water access and food security indicates that their burdens will increase disproportionately due to climate change.

84.7% of women in Rwanda depend on firewood as their main source of cooking energy, largely due to limited accessibility and affordability to the high cost of alternative sources of energy (i.e., cooking gas and biogas). As a result of social cultural norms, women are responsible for collecting firewood from forests, which is unsafe for providing cooking fuel in their homes. Limited access to alternative energy sources increases their workload and limits their engagement in other productive activities, compounding their climate vulnerability.

### 4. Policy and Strategy Environment

#### 4.1 Institutional Framework for Gender Mainstreaming in Rwanda

In addition to some of the key legal reforms noted above, Rwanda’s policy and strategy environment is in many ways considered favourable for promoting and mainstreaming gender equality and the participation and leadership of women and girls. There are a number of national policies and strategies that support this goal and affirm the equal rights of women and girls, both as a targeted priority as well as a cross-cutting theme.

#### 4.1.1 Vision 2050

Vision 2050 serves as an extension and refocusing of the previous Vision 2020, with the aim of Rwanda achieving upper middle-income status by 2035 and high-income status by 2060. Some of the target goals of Vision 2050 include universal secondary education, better health outcomes, sustainable and reliable household access to improved water and sanitation, and universal access to quality health care services. Both Vision 2020 and Vision 2050 highlight gender equality and women’s empowerment as an area of critical and cross-cutting importance.


---
**Page 115**
---

4.1.2 National Strategy for Transformation (NST1)
Rwanda’s National Strategy for Transformation (2017-2034) provides overarching guidance and focal areas for the accelerated social, political and economic transformation of the country. The NST1 includes various different strategies to address gender equality and foster women's empowerment, including mainstreaming gender in employment and job creation, improving women's access to finance and continuing to combat gender-based violence (GBV).

4.1.3 Strategic Plan for Agriculture Transformation 2018-2024 (PSATAV)
The Strategic Plan for Agricultural Transformation on the mechanization and commercialization of the agriculture sector in Rwanda, as a necessary precursor for poverty reduction and economic growth. The plan includes specific strategies to address gender inequality in the agriculture sector. It is further complemented by the Agriculture Sector Development Strategy 2018-2034 which provides a gender analysis of the agriculture sector and mainstreaming guidance for greater gender equality and women's participation and empowerment across all government agricultural programs and projects.

4.1.4 Revised National Gender Policy 2021
Rwanda’s National Gender Policy, launched by the Ministry of Gender and Family Promotion (MIGFAP), was recently revised in February 2021. The new policy specifically addresses a number of key gender issues, including the burden of unpaid care work and its impact on women's health, well-being and economic independence, as well as the importance of women being involved in climate adaptation and mitigation strategies and solutions.

4.1.5 National Land Policy (2019)
This policy ensures that land rights for all citizens (women and men) are in place and enforced. Since the recent land registration in 2013, there has been some improvement in gender parity with respect to land ownership between men and women. Currently, 59.9% of registered land is owned by married couples, 24% is owned by women and 16% is owned by men.

4.1.6 National Environment and Climate Change Policy 2019
One of the formal guiding principles of the National Environment and Climate Change Policy is “industrialization with environmental sustainability” which focuses on environmental management and climate change intervention decision-making, and promoting the targeted involvement of women and youth in developing green technologies and designing climate change adaptation and mitigation solutions.

4.1.7 National Housing Policy 2015
This policy highlights the importance of including women and other marginalized groups in socio-economic development as well as improving their access to resources and off-farm jobs. The policy also emphasizes the need to encourage female participation in the design of neighborhoods and settlements, and in the planning of social amenities and facilities.

4.1.8 National Urbanization Policy 2015
Within the National Urbanization Policy, Guiding Principle #8 underscores the importance of gender-sensitive commitments in Rwanda’s approach to urbanization. Gender equality and women's empowerment are central themes within the policy, including the importance of supporting women as part of the urban economic force, promoting women's and girls' participation in urban planning, design


---
**Page 116**
---

and management at city and neighbourhood levels, and strengthening the prevention of gender-based violence in urban areas.

### Sectoral Policy on Water and Sanitation 2004

Sitting under the Ministry of Environment, this policy acknowledges the insufficient participation of women in water infrastructure, the need for women and men to equally participate in water resource protection and management, and the need for the integration of a gender perspective into all levels of water management.

While the overall policy and strategy framework for gender equality in Rwanda is generally supportive, there are notable exceptions, such as the National Informal Urban Settlement Upgrading Strategy of 2017, which does not acknowledge or analyze the unique situation of women and girls in urban settlements, or make any explicit mention of the importance of gender equality and the empowerment and equal participation of women and girls in all matters related to urban settlement upgrades.

### The Green Climate Fund Gender Policy

The GCF Gender Policy outlines the GCF’s clear commitments to ensuring effective gender mainstreaming across its operations and funded projects. The GCF commits to consistently mainstreaming gender issues in its implementation arrangements as well as frameworks for its projects.[37]

#### The Gender Policy of the GCF (reviewed and updated in November 2019) explicitly recognizes that gender relations, roles and responsibilities exercise significant influence on women’s and men’s access to and control over resources, including information and knowledge. The Policy also recognizes that the impacts of climate change can exacerbate existing gender inequalities, and acknowledges that climate change initiatives are more sustainable, equitable and more likely to achieve their objectives when gender equality and women's empowerment considerations are integrated into the design and implementation of projects. The updated GCF Gender Policy also states that women and vulnerable communities are part of the solution to climate change and should, therefore, be effectively engaged in all discussions and decisions that affect them.[38]

The GCF Gender Policy is closely aligned with the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), which make explicit commitments for gender equality as a starting point on gender equality and women’s empowerment (SEW) and as a cross-cutting theme of the SDG agenda. The Gender Policy is guided by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, which acknowledges that in the process of addressing climate change, principles of equity and the special needs of women should be respected, promoted and applied. The Gender Policy is aligned with the Paris Agreement, which reflects the commitment by Parties that adaptation action should be “gender-responsive”, and when taking action to address climate change, Parties must respect, promote and consider their respective obligations under international law, including international human rights law, local communities, migrants, children, persons with disabilities and people in vulnerable situations and the

### 5. Ngaruyinka Village Upgrade: Overview and Proposed Interventions

#### 5.1 Village overview
Two pilot projects have been identified to be taken forward in the first phase of the implementation process for Green City Kigali. They are located close to each other in the north-western corner of Kinyinya Hill, creating opportunities for shared infrastructure and enabling works. Both pilot projects are founded on the four pillars of sustainable development and will deliver on the overall objectives of the GCK project. The two pilot projects are an 18 ha urban upgrade of an existing informal settlement in Ngaruyinka village.

The Ngaruyinka village pilot has been selected to provide a model for climate responsive upgrading of an informal settlement. The objective is to create a model approach which can be upscaled and applied to multiple situations across Rwanda to maximize positive climate impacts on both men and women in line with the GCK approach.

Ngaruyinka village lies on a steep north-facing slope. For the most part the land falls to the north at a gradient of around 15%. The slope gets shallower at the southern and northern ends and it steepens in the central area. Building foundations, roads and pathways have been manually levelled. Most buildings have poured concrete foundations, and some have concrete retaining walls. Roads and pathways are often finished with gravel or crushed stone retaining walls.

The resident population of the Ngaruyinka Village study area has been calculated at 1,634 persons. This has been calculated through a house-by-house count undertaken by a local survey team with assistance from community leaders and representatives. The average household size is 4-5 persons. The oldest living person in Ngaruyinka is 90 years old. The built footprint of the village consists mainly of single family and multi-family dwellings, covering an area of approximately 321 ha. There are two primary commercial areas at the foot of the hill along the main access road and a secondary commercial area within the village itself.

There is a nursery school located halfway up the slope on the western boundary of the village, adjacent to the Ministry of Health site. Primary and Secondary schools are located in Kinyinya centre. Muruma sector has been proposed as an area that could be upgraded by Green City Kigali including Ngaruyinka. There are three community health workers in the village. The closest Primary Health Clinic is Kinyinya Health Centre about a 40-minute walk away, and there is a health post in Ruanda ruka about a 20-minute walk from Ngaruyinka. It can be reached every morning by truck from Kinyinya center and consists of the following local shops and services:

- 2 x small restaurants serving local food; milk and tea
- A small market square selling locally harvested vegetables and charcoal
- A lunch tenten serving tea and bread
- A men's toilet (toilet block for women)
- Several small shops and kiosks selling vegetables, milk, grains
- Butcher shop (opening soon)


---
**Page 118**
---

Local community meetings and events are often held under trees in a clearing in the middle of the village which is also used as a market place. The New Life in Jesus church lies about 30min walk away from Ngaryinya. Other religious buildings are located up the hill about 25 minutes walk away, toward Kinyinya Centre. Virtually the entire 18ha study area is within a 200m catchment of either a commercial area or the nursery school. The remainder of the area is a mix of grassland, formal farms and informal, domestic level farming. Further development in the village has been halted until infrastructure can be improved.

Most of the buildings in the village are single story homes and/or commercial premises constructed of mud/clay bricks, typically with corrugated steel roofs and steel framed doors and windows. Many buildings have concrete foundations and facing render. There are occasional concrete block buildings. Many buildings lack drainage systems or rely on natural drainage features, particularly those on high erosion risk spots. For the most part, many streets and pathways in the village are rarely trafficked and in effect act as pedestrian/costumed public space and play area. There are a few specific places that are regularly used for public gatherings and social activities such as the church.

Every house located along the streets and pathways in the village is required to have a light facing the street to act as public lighting, though coverage is very limited. There is no evidence of formal street furniture or public waste bins in the village.


Approximately 60% of the population of the village rent their homes. It is typical that the landowners are also the landlords and the rental agreements are not regulated by law. The study area was based on interviews with local residents at Ngaryinya Village, most crimes in the area are petty thefts of phones, bags, etc. There is a community patrol in the village that is organised by a community member responsible for security.


There are significant existing environmental and social risks in Ngaryinya village. The increase in informal neighbourhood, a direct consequence of urbanisation, is largely due to market forces in the inner cities. Low-income earners are continually being pushed out of Kigali's city centre due to the high cost of living. It has been estimated that more than 340,000 housing units will be needed by 2022 to supply the demand for new homes. The lack of affordable housing in the inner cities and its effects has driven the development of many informal enclosures where housing structures are of poor quality. In Ngaryinya village, this informal development is characterised by a lack of adequate waste and sanitation facilities, which results in environmental degradation, gaps leading to flooding, and increased social risks, such as poorer health and vulnerability to climate events. The High-Level Environmental and Social Impact Assessment Report prepared for the Green City Kigali Project contains additional information regarding potential environmental and social impacts.

The table below briefly outlines the different interventions proposed for the village upgrade. Discussions with men and women in Ngaryinya village explored the perceptions of different community members related to the proposed interventions detailed below.

## 6. Methodology of the Gender Assessment for Ngaruyinka Village

### 6.1 Baseline Data Collection

The gender assessment conducted in Ngaruyinka village adopted an interactive and participatory approach, engaging both women and men of different ages and backgrounds to gather qualitative data on the roles, responsibilities, and challenges faced by each group within the context of their daily lives and infrastructure differences. Data was collected from a diverse range of women and men, including married women and men, single women and men, young women and men, elderly women and men, adolescents, people with disabilities, pregnant women, new mothers, traditional healers, health workers, and those from different ubicube categories. Names of participants have been removed and any identifying details have been otherwise made ambiguous or generalized in order to protect the anonymity of respondents.

Baseline data collection began on September 6th, 2021 and was completed on September 27th, 2021. A total of seven site visits were conducted that captured detailed feedback from community members, involving focus group discussions and in-depth individual interviews. Informed, written consent was collected from all participants before data collection proceeded.

18


---
**Page 120**
---

# Focus group discussions and interviews gathered data across the following themes:

- How women and men spend their time on a daily basis
- How women and men use village spaces, infrastructure and utilities differently
- Gender dynamics within the village, at the community level
- Gender dynamics within the village, at the household level
- Women's participation in leadership and decision making in the home and community
- Women's role in planning and decision-making related to the village upgrade consultations and planning process
- Women's and men's perceptions of the proposed upgrade interventions
- Women's public safety in the village

## 6.2 Focus group discussions

Focus group discussions (FGDs) were conducted with both men and women to assess their different experiences and perspectives. Four focus groups were conducted with women, and two with men; each FGD focused on different thematicatics and utilized a variety of approaches for gathering data related to gender dynamics in the village as well as different perceptions of the proposed upgrade interventions.

Focus group discussions were conducted with women from different ages and backgrounds, including adolescent girls, community health workers and widows/women-headed households, to dig deeper into key gender issues from their unique perspectives and gather individual feedback on the proposed interventions for the upgrade.

## 6.3 In-depth interviews

Three further in-depth additional interviews were conducted with women and girls of different ages and backgrounds, including adolescent girls, community health workers and widows/women-headed households, to dig deeper into key gender issues from their unique perspectives and gather individual feedback on the proposed interventions for the upgrade.
